The geometric data on the boundary of convex subsets of hyperbolic manifolds Thanks: Q. Chen was partially supported by NSFC No: 12101244, Guangdong Basic and Applied Basic Research Foundation No: 2022A1515012225, and Science and Technology Program of Guangzhou, China No: 202201010464. Thanks: J.-M. S. was partially supported by FNR projects INTER/ANR/15/11211745 and O20/14766753.
Qiyu Chen, Jean-Marc Schlenker

Abstract
. Let N N be a geodesically convex subset in a convex co-compact hyperbolic manifold M M with incompressible boundary. We assume that each boundary component of N N is either a boundary component of ∂ ∞ M \partial_{\infty}M , or a smooth, locally convex surface in M M . We show that N N is uniquely determined by the boundary data defined by the conformal structure on the boundary components at infinity, and by either the induced metric or the third fundamental form on the boundary components which are locally convex surfaces. We also describe the possible boundary data. This provides an extension of both the hyperbolic Weyl problem and the Ahlfors-Bers Theorem. Using this statement for quasifuchsian manifolds, we obtain existence results for similar questions for convex domains Ω ⊂ ℍ 3 \Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3} which meets the boundary at infinity ∂ ∞ ℍ 3 \partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3} either along a quasicircle or along a quasidisk. The boundary data then includes either the induced metric or the third fundamental form in ℍ 3 {\mathbb{H}}^{3} , but also an additional “gluing” data between different components of the boundary, either in ℍ 3 {\mathbb{H}}^{3} or in ∂ ∞ ℍ 3 \partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3} . Keywords: hyperbolic geometry, Weyl problem, convex surfaces, isometric embeddings.



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
The classical Weyl problem in Euclidean and hyperbolic spaces

The classical Weyl problem, going back to Hermann Weyl
[
48
]
, asks whether any smooth metric of positive curvature on the sphere can be obtained uniquely as the induced metric on the boundary of a bounded convex subset in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. A proof was obtained by Lewy
[
27
]
for analytic metrics, and later by Alexandrov
[
4
]
for polyhedral metrics. The result was then further extended by Pogorelov
[
31
]
to non-smooth metrics: the induced metric on the boundary of any bounded convex subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(even if non-smooth) is a geodesic distance of curvature
K\geq 0
in the sense of Alexandrov, and each such metric on the sphere is realized on the boundary of a unique bounded convex body.

The same question was considered by Alexandrov
[
4
]
and Pogorelov
[
31
]
for surfaces in the 3-dimensional hyperbolic space
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. Given a bounded convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the induced metric on its boundary has curvature
K\geq-1
. Conversely, Alexandrov and Pogorelov proved that any smooth metric of curvature
K>-1
is induced on the boundary of a unique bounded convex subset of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with smooth boundary. A similar result holds for bounded polyhedra, for which the induced metric on the boundary is a hyperbolic metric with cone singularities of angles less than
2\pi

[
4
]
.

In the hyperbolic space, there is also a
dual
Weyl problem, describing the possible third fundamental forms of the boundaries of bounded convex subsets. For a smooth surfaces
S\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the third fundamental form is classically defined for two tangent vectors
u,v
to
S
at a point
x
by:

I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI(u,v)=I(\nabla_{u}N,\nabla_{v}N),

where
I
is the induced metric on
S
,
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, and
N
is a unit normal vector field to
S
. This third fundamental form can also be described as the induced metric on a dual surface in the de Sitter space (see Section
2.6
), and under this definition extends to the boundary of a convex domain with non-smooth boundary, for instance polyhedra in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. For polyhedra in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the third fundamental form is also called the
dual metric
, e.g. in
[
23
]
.

If
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is a convex bounded domain, the third fundamental form on its boundary is
CAT(1)
: it has curvature
K\leq 1
, and its closed geodesics have length
L>2\pi

[
15
]
. Conversely, any smooth metric on
S^{2}
with curvature
K<1
and closed geodesics of length
L>2\pi
is realized as the third fundamental form on the boundary of a unique bounded convex subset in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with smooth boundary, see
[
37
]
. A similar result was proved by Rivin and Hodgson
[
32
,
23
]
: any spherical metric on
S^{2}
with cone singularities of angles greater than
2\pi
, with closed geodesics of length
L>2\pi
, is the dual metric of a unique bounded polyhedron in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. This description of the third fundamental forms on the boundary of bounded or ideal polyhedra is also related to the description of the dihedral angles of those polyhedra, see e.g.
[
6
,
7
,
38
,
33
,
35
]
.

1.2.
The Weyl problem for unbounded convex subsets in hyperbolic space

We are interested here in the Weyl problem for convex subsets that are not bounded. More specifically, we will consider here what are perhaps the simplest cases of such a convex subset, of two possible types. The first case is a convex unbounded subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
such that

•

the boundary of
\Omega
in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is an open disk, which we denote by
\partial\Omega
,

•

the ideal boundary of
\Omega
in the ideal boundary
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(identified with
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
) of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is a closed disk, which we denote by
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
.

Clearly, in this setting, the induced metric (or third fundamental form) on
\partial\Omega
does not uniquely determine
\Omega
(up to isometries). For instance, if
\Omega
is the hyperbolic convex hull of
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
(assuming that
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
is not a round disk), then
\partial\Omega
is developable, and
(\partial\Omega,I)
is always isometric to the hyperbolic plane. In the same setting, for any
K\in(-1,0)
, the boundary (in
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
) of
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
bounds a unique
K
-surface
\Sigma_{K}
bounding a convex domain with asymptotic boundary
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, see
[
34
]
, and
\Sigma_{K}
equipped with its induced metric is always isometric to the hyperbolic plane with the metric scaled by
1/|K|
. The same construction can also be used for the third fundamental form, since
\Sigma_{K}
also has a third fundamental form of constant curvature
K^{*}=K/(K+1)
, so it is always isometric to the hyperbolic plane with the metric scaled by
1/|K^{*}|
.

The second type of unbounded convex subset of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
that we consider here is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
such that
\partial\Omega
is the disjoint union of two complete disks, denoted here by
\partial_{-}\Omega
and
\partial_{+}\Omega
, which “meet” at infinity along a Jordan curve. In the spirit of the Weyl problem and its dual, we would like to prescribe on
\partial_{-}\Omega
and
\partial_{+}\Omega
either the induced metric or the third fundamental form. However this data is again not sufficient to uniquely determine
\Omega
, as shown in
[
10
]
for metrics of constant curvature: one needs also to prescribe a “gluing map” at infinity between
\partial_{-}\Omega
and
\partial_{+}\Omega
.

Our results are obtained only when the limit curve at infinity – the boundary of
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
in the first case, the common boundary of
\partial_{-}\Omega
and
\partial_{+}\Omega
in the second case – is a quasi-circle, which corresponds to the gluing maps being quasi-symmetric. We can state those results as follows, with points (i) and (ii) corresponding to the first case above, and points (iii)-(v) corresponding to the second case. Before stating the result, however, we need a simple definition.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
h
be a conformal metric on the open unit disk
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, and let
h_{0}
be the conformal hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
. We say that
h
is bounded if there exists a constant
C>1
such that at all points of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
,
h\leq Ch_{0}
.

Note that if
h
is complete and has curvature
K\geq-c
for some
c>0
, then
h\geq(1/c)h_{0}

(see
[
29
, Theorem 2.8]
, where the argument is based on
[
16
]
).

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
D_{-},D_{+}\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
be disjoint quasi-disks with
\partial D_{-}=\partial D_{+}
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and let
h_{-}
(resp.
h_{+}
) be a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metric of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
D_{-}
(resp.
D_{+}
), and
h^{*}_{-}
(resp.
h^{*}_{+}
) be a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metric of curvature varying in
[-1/\epsilon,0]
on
D_{-}
(resp.
D_{+}
). Then

(i)

There exists a convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
a closed disk and a continuous map
\phi:{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial_{\infty}\Omega
sending
D_{+}
to
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, which is conformal on
D_{+}
and such that
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
.

(ii)

There exists a convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
a closed disk and a continuous map
\phi:{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial_{\infty}\Omega
sending
D_{+}
to
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, which is conformal on
D_{+}
and such that
\phi^{*}I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI=h^{*}_{-}
on
D_{-}
.

(iii)

There exists a convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
a Jordan curve, and a continuous map
\phi:{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, such that
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
and
\phi^{*}I=h_{+}
on
D_{+}
.

(iv)

There exists a convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
a Jordan curve, and a continuous map
\phi:{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, such that
\phi^{*}I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI=h^{*}_{-}
on
D_{-}
and
\phi^{*}I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI=h^{*}_{+}
on
D_{+}
.

(v)

There exists a convex subset
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
a Jordan curve, and a continuous map
\phi:{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\to\partial\Omega\cup\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, such that
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
and
\phi^{*}I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI=h_{+}^{*}
on
D_{+}
.

The hypothesis on the metrics
h^{*}_{\pm}
are probably not optimal – one could expect similar results only assuming that those metrics have curvature in
[-1/\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
, and that their closed geodesics have length
L>2\pi
. However the arguments used in Section
5
only function (at this point) with the stronger assumption considered here. The hypothesis that those metrics are “bounded” in the sense considered here is also necessary for “technical” reasons in the proofs.

Theorem
1.2
extends the main result of
[
10
]
,
which dealt only with constant curvature metrics, and has a non-empty intersection with the main result of
[
43
]
, which only deals with the induced metric. However it should be pointed out that the argument used here is quite different, since the approximation was done in
[
43
]
by metrics on the sphere, while here we use a quasifuchsian approximation argument. One could also find additional motivations in
[
42
]
.

1.3.
Convex domains in quasifuchsian manifolds

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is based on an approximation argument, where complete metrics on a disk together with quasi-symmetric homeomorphisms from
{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
to itself are approximated, in a suitable sense, by metrics and quasi-symmetric homeomorphisms which are invariant (resp. equivariant) with respect to the action of surface groups.
Those invariant metrics (and equivariant gluing maps) correspond to convex surfaces embedded in quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifolds.

In this quasifuchsian setting, and more generally in the convex co-compact setting discussed below, we will obtain an existence but also a uniqueness statement. We first discuss the quasifuchsian setting here for simplicity, and for reference below.

Let
S
be a closed, oriented connected surface of genus at least
2
and let
{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
denote the Teichmüller space of
S
, which is the space of hyperbolic metrics (or conformal structures) on
S
(up to isotopies). The following statement is a consequence of Theorem
1.6
below.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
h
be a Riemannian metric of curvature greater than
-1
on
S
, and let
c\in{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
be a conformal structure on
S
. Then there is a unique (up to isotopy) hyperbolic manifold homeomorphic to
S\times[0,+\infty)
which has a convex boundary at
S\times\{0\}
with induced metric isotopic to
h
, and has a conformal structure at infinity on
S\times\{+\infty\}
isotopic to
c
.

We now consider a “dual” statement to Corollary
1.3
, where we prescribe the third fundamental form rather than the induced metric.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
h^{*}
be a Riemannian metric of curvature less than
1
on
S
with closed, contractible geodesics of length greater than
2\pi
, and let
c\in{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
be a conformal structure on
S
. Then there is a unique (up to isotopy) hyperbolic manifold homeomorphic to
S\times[0,+\infty)
which has a convex boundary at
S\times\{0\}
with the third fundamental form isotopic to
h^{*}
, and has a conformal structure at infinity on
S\times\{+\infty\}
isotopic to
c
.

Note that the above hyperbolic manifold can be isometrically embedded in a unique (up to isometries) quasifuchsian manifold.

A consequence of Corollary
1.3
concerns quasifuchsian manifolds with induced metric prescribed on one side of the convex core, while the conformal structure at infinity is prescribed on the other side. We only claim existence in that statement, since proving uniqueness would request obtaining a local rigidity statement that appears difficult. The statement is presented here as a theorem since it is not a special case of Theorem
1.6
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
h\in{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
be a hyperbolic metric on
S
, and let
c\in{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
be a conformal structure on
S
.
Then there is a quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold with the conformal structure on its upper boundary component at infinity isotopic to
c
, and with the induced metric on the lower boundary component of its convex core isotopic to
h
.

1.4.
Convex domains in convex co-compact manifolds

We finally state a general result on the existence and uniqueness of convex co-compact hyperbolic manifold containing a geodesically convex subset such that the induced metric, third fundamental form, or conformal metric at infinity is prescribed for each end.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
M
be a compact 3-manifold with non-empty, incompressible boundary which admits a convex co-compact hyperbolic structure on its interior and let
\partial_{l}M
(
l=1,\ldots,n
,
n\geq 2
) denote its boundary components. Let
\mathcal{I}\sqcup\mathcal{J}\sqcup\mathcal{K}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
be a partition, where
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{J}
, or
\mathcal{K}
might be an empty set. Let
c_{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
) be a conformal structure on
\partial_{i}M
,
h_{j}
(
j\in\mathcal{J}
) be a Riemannian metric of curvature greater than
-1
on
\partial_{j}M
and
h_{k}^{*}
(
k\in\mathcal{K}
) be a Riemannian metric of curvature less than
1
on
\partial_{k}M
, with closed, contractible geodesics of length greater than
2\pi
. Then there exists a unique (up to isotopy) convex co-compact hyperbolic structure on
M
which is complete on
\partial_{i}M
(if
\mathcal{I}\not=\emptyset
) with conformal structure isotopic to
c_{i}
, and with induced metric isotopic to
h_{j}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{j}M
(if
\mathcal{J}\not=\emptyset
), and with third fundamental form isotopic to
h_{k}^{*}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{k}M
(if
\mathcal{K}\not=\emptyset
).

Corollaries
1.3
and
1.4
clearly follow from this theorem.

2.
Background material

2.1.
The hyperbolic and de Sitter spaces

Recall that the hyperboloid models of the 3-dimensional hyperbolic space and de Sitter (dS) space are respectively

H^{3}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}|\langle x,x\rangle_{3,1}=-1,x_{0}>0\},

and

dS^{3}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}|\langle x,x\rangle_{3,1}=1\},

where
\langle x,y\rangle_{3,1}=-x_{0}y_{0}+x_{1}y_{1}+x_{2}y_{2}+x_{3}y_{3}
. We denote by
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
) the projection of
H^{3}
(resp.
dS^{3}
) to
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
(with the metric induced from the projection), which is the
projective model
of the hyperbolic (resp. dS) 3-space (we use this model throughout the paper, unless otherwise stated).

Let
\mathbb{HS}^{3}
denote the space
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
equipped with the geometric structure induced by the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{3,1}
, called the 3-dimensional
HS space of signature
(3,0)
. By construction its geometry is invariant under
PO_{0}(3,1)
. It has a component - corresponding to the time-like vectors with
x_{0}>0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
isometric to
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, while the complement of the closure of this component is isometric to
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
. The signature of the metric induced on
\mathbb{HS}^{3}
is in fact
(3,0)
on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and
(2,1)
on
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.

Let
\phi:{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\cup{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
be a local section of the canonical projection
\pi:{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
with
\phi({\mathbb{H}}^{3})=H^{3}
and
\phi({{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3})\subset dS^{3}
. For each point
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\cup{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
, we define the
HS scalar product
on
T_{x}\mathbb{HS}^{3}
as

\langle v,w\rangle_{HS}:=\langle\phi_{*}(v),\phi_{*}(w)\rangle_{3,1}

for all
v,w\in T_{x}\mathbb{HS}^{3}
. The restriction of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{HS}
to
T{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
T{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
) exactly induces the hyperbolic (resp. dS) metric.

The boundary in
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
or
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
coincides with the projectivization in
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
of the set of light-like vectors in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
, which is conformally equivalent to
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
in a canonical way. Let
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}={\mathbb{H}}^{3}\cup{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
denote the natural (conformal) compactification of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
by
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
. Note that the action of isometries of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
can extend to
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
as projective automorphisms (or Möbius transformations) and conversely, Möbius transformations on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
can extend to isometries inside
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(using the Poincaré upper half-space model)
[
28
]
. For simplicity, we denote by
{\rm PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
the group of orientation-preserving isometries of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
as well as the group of Möbius transformations on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
. Let
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the Lie algebra of
{\rm PSL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})}
, which can be interpreted as the space of infinitesimal automorphisms of
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
. These are vector fields on
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
whose flows are isometries (resp. Möbius transformations) on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
\partial\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\cong{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
), called
Killing
(resp.
\emph{projective}
) vector fields (see e.g.
[
22
,
12
]
).

2.2.
The
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-bundle over
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}

Let
E
denote the trivial
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-bundle over
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
. Let
\kappa\in\Gamma(E)
be a section of
E
. If
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the value of
\kappa
at
x
, denoted by
\kappa_{x}
, is a Killing vector field on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, which has the following decomposition (see e.g.
[
22
,
40
]
):

\kappa_{x}:=u_{x}+v_{x},

where
u_{x}
is the
infinitesimal pure translation at
x
(namely,
u_{x}(x)=\kappa_{x}(x)
and
\nabla_{X}u_{x}=0
for all
X\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
), while
v_{x}
is the
infinitesimal pure rotation at
x
(namely,
v_{x}(x)=0
and there exists a vector say
\sigma(x)\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
such that
\nabla_{X}v_{x}=\sigma(x)\times X
for all
X\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
), where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. As a consequence, we can identify
\kappa_{x}
with a pair of tangent vectors at
x
, say
(\tau(x),\sigma(x))\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\times T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, which satisfy that

\tau(x)=\kappa_{x}(x),\quad\quad\nabla_{X}\kappa_{x}=\sigma(x)\times X,

for all
X\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. The
curl
of a vector field
X
on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is defined as

(1)

{\rm curl}X:=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/3{\mathbb{Z}}}(\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}X,e_{i+1}\rangle-\langle\nabla_{e_{i+1}}X,e_{i}\rangle)e_{i+2},

where
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
is an orthonormal frame of
T{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(see e.g.
[
45
, Section 11.1]
). A direct computation shows that the above
\sigma(x)
is one half the curl of
\kappa_{x}
at
x
, similarly for the euclidean case in Section
2.4
.

In this way, the restriction to
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of a section
\kappa\in\Gamma(E)
is identified with a pair of vector fields
(\tau,\sigma)\in\Gamma(T{\mathbb{H}}^{3})\times\Gamma(T{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
. For convenience, we call
\tau
(resp.
\sigma
) the
translation
(resp.
rotation
)
component
of
\kappa
. Under this identification and by a direct computation of the flat connection
D
for
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
-bundle over
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, we conclude that: for any
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and any
X\in T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the one-form
\omega=d^{D}\kappa
can be written as (see
[
22
]
or
[
40
, Section 3]
):

(2)

\omega(X)=d^{D}_{X}(\tau,\sigma)=\big(\nabla_{X}\tau-\sigma(x)\times X,\nabla_{X}\sigma+\tau(x)\times X\big)=:\big(\omega_{\tau}(X),\omega_{\sigma}(X)\big).

We call
\omega_{\tau}
(resp.
\omega_{\sigma}
) the
translation
(resp.
rotation
)
part
of
\omega
.
Note that
\kappa
is a constant section if and only if
d^{D}\kappa=0
, also, if and only if
\tau
and
\sigma
are hyperbolic Killing fields.

If
x\in{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
,
\kappa_{x}
is a projective vector field on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
. Let
\big(\phi_{t}(z)\big)_{t\in[0,\varepsilon]}
be a one-parameter family of Möbius transformations which generate
\kappa_{x}
. By computing the derivative at
t=0
of
\phi_{t}(z)
,
\kappa_{x}
has the following form in an affine chart
z
near
x
(see e.g.
[
14
, Section 3.2]
and
[
30
, Remark 4.4]
):

\kappa_{x}(z)=P(z)\partial_{z},

where
P(z)
is a complex polynomial of degree at most 2 (called
quadratic polynomials
for simplicity). Moreover, there is a unique Killing field on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, determined by the above
(\phi_{t}(z))_{t\in[0,\varepsilon]}
, that extends continuously to
\kappa_{x}
(see the proof of Lemma
3.14
for instance).

2.3.
The convex co-compact structures and their deformations

Let
M
be a compact 3-manifold with non-empty, incompressible boundary. A
({\rm PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}}),\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}})
-structure
on
M
is a maximal atlas of charts
\{(U_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})\}
to
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
with transition maps the restrictions of elements of
{\rm PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, which is a
hyperbolic structure
when restricted to the interior of
M
, and a
complex projective structure
when restricted to
\partial M
(see e.g.
[
12
]
). Under this condition, the interior of
M
is equipped with a complete, convex co-compact hyperbolic metric, also called a
convex co-compact structure
. (To see this, let
(D_{i})_{i=1,\dots,n}
be a cover of
\partial M
by embedded open disks, for the complex projective structure at infinity. Each of the
D_{i}
bounds a half-space
H_{i}
embedded in
M
. The complement
\Omega
in
M
of the union of the
H_{i}
is then by construction a non-empty, geodesically convex subset of
M
, in the sense that any geodesic segment in
M
with endpoints in
\Omega
is contained in
\Omega
. This proves that the hyperbolic structure on the interior of
M
is convex co-compact.)

Let
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
denote the space of convex co-compact structures on
M
(up to isotopy). In particular, if
M
is homeomorphic to the product of a surface
S
(of genus at least
2
) by an interval,
the manifold
M
equipped with a convex co-compact structure is also called a
quasifuchsian
hyperbolic manifold. In this case, the two boundary components of
M
are homeomorphic to
S
.

2.3.1.
The deformation vector field

Let
(U_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha}^{t})_{t\in[0,\epsilon]}
be a smooth one-parameter family of convex co-compact structures on
M
. Let
dev_{t}
and
hol_{t},t\in[0,\epsilon]
, be the corresponding developing maps and holonomy representations, respectively, where
dev_{t}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
and
hol_{t}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow{\rm PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
with
\tilde{M}
the universal cover of
M
. This defines a
deformation vector field
say
u
, on
\tilde{M}
, which associates to each
x\in\tilde{M}
a tangent vector at
t=0
, say
u(x)
, of the curve
t\mapsto dev_{0}^{-1}(dev_{t}(x))
. The equivariance property
dev_{t}(\gamma\cdot x)=hol_{t}(\gamma)\circ dev_{t}(x)
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
implies that the vector field
\gamma_{*}u-u
is
Killing
(resp.
projective
) on
\tilde{M}\setminus\partial\tilde{M}
(resp.
\partial\tilde{M}
):

(3)

(\gamma_{*})^{-1}u(\gamma\cdot x)=u(x)+dev_{0}^{*}\big(\dot{hol}_{0}(\gamma)(dev_{0}(x))\big),

where
\dot{hol}_{0}(\gamma)\in sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
is the derivative of
hol_{t}(\gamma)
at
t=0
, which is a Killing (resp. projective) vector field on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
). We call such
u

automorphic
. Moreover, we say
u
is
equivariant
(with respect to the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
) if in particular
\dot{hol}_{0}(\gamma)=0
(in this case,
dev_{t}
is obtained by pre-composing
dev_{0}
with the lift of diffeomorphisms
f_{t}
of
M
isotopic to the identity), see e.g.
[
22
,
14
,
30
]

Note that if the above first-order deformation preserves the conformal structure at infinity on
\partial_{l}M
(
1\leq l\leq n
), then
u
is a conformal vector field when restricted to
\partial_{l}\tilde{M}
.

2.3.2.
The deformation section

Let
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
be the limit set of the Klein group
hol_{0}(\pi_{1}(M))
. For simplicity, we identify the universal cover
\tilde{M}
with its image
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
under the developing map. Now we introduce a
deformation section
of
E
over
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda
(associated to a deformation vector field
u
) as follows (see
[
14
,
30
]
).

For
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, we define a Killing vector field

s_{x}\in sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
that “best approximates” the vector field
u
at
x
:

(4)

s_{x}(x)=u(x),\quad\quad{\rm{curl}}(s_{x})(x)={\rm curl}(u)(x).

For
z_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus\Lambda
, let
u(z)=f(z)\partial_{z}
be the local expression of
u
in an affine chart near
z_{0}
, we define a projective vector field
s_{z_{0}}
whose two-jets agree with
f
at
z_{0}
. More precisely,

(5)

s_{z_{0}}(z)=\big(f(z_{0})+f_{z}(z_{0})(z-z_{0})+f_{zz}(z_{0})\frac{(z-z_{0})^{2}}{2}\big)\partial_{z}.

Note that the above
s_{x}
(resp.
s_{z_{0}}
) is uniquely determined by the equations (
4
) (resp. the equation (
5
)). We call such a section the
canonical lift
of
u
and denote it by
s_{u}
. By definition, the canonical lift of a vector field in
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
is a constant section. As a consequence, the canonical lift
s_{u}
is
automorphic
, that is, for each
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
,
(s_{u})_{\gamma\cdot x}-\gamma_{*}(s_{u})_{x}
is the same element in
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
for all
x\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda
. In particular, if
u
is equivariant, then
s_{u}
is
equivariant
, in the sense that
(s_{u})_{\gamma\cdot x}=\gamma_{*}(s_{u})_{x}
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
and
x\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda
.

2.3.3.
The deformation one-form

Recall that
E
has a natural flat connection
D
. Let
d^{D}
be the de Rham differential twisted by
D
. We now introduce a
deformation one-form
:
\tilde{\omega}=d^{D}s_{u}
on
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda
, see e.g.
[
22
,
14
,
30
]
. One can check that
\tilde{\omega}
is an
equivariant

E
-valued one-form, which measures how far the deformation vector field
u
differs from a Killing (resp. projective) field on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
). Indeed,

\tilde{\omega}-\gamma_{*}\tilde{\omega}=d^{D}s_{u}-\gamma_{*}d^{D}s_{u}=d^{D}(s_{u}-\gamma_{*}s_{u})=0.

So
\tilde{\omega}
descends to an
E_{M}
-valued closed one-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M,E_{M})
on
M
(since it is locally exact), where
E_{M}
is the quotient of
(\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda)\times sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
by
hol_{0}(\pi_{1}(M))
, which acts on
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\setminus\Lambda
by deck transformations and on
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
via adjoint representation.

A first-order deformation of a convex co-compact structure
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
is
trivial
if the corresponding deformation vector field
u
belongs to
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
or is equivariant, or equivalently, the deformation section
s_{u}
is constant or equivariant, or equivalently, the deformation one-form
\tilde{\omega}=d^{D}s_{u}
is exact. As a consequence, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the space of infinitesimal deformations of
M
modulo trivial deformations and the first-cohomology group
H^{1}(M,E_{M})
(see
[
22
,
14
,
30
]
).

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\kappa
be a Killing vector field on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(which can be viewed as a constant section of
E
over
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
). We define the norm of
\kappa
at
x
as

\|\kappa\|_{x}:=\|\tau(x)\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}+\|\sigma(x)\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}},

where
\tau
and
\sigma
are respectively the translation and rotation components of
\kappa
. Let
\omega\in H^{1}(M,E_{M})
. The norm of
\omega
at
p\in M
is defined as

\|\omega\|_{p}:=\sup_{X\in T_{p}M:\,\|X\|_{T_{p}M}=1}\|\omega(X)\|_{p},

where
\omega(X)\in E_{M}
is a local Killing field on a neighbourhood in
M
of
p
. We say
\omega
is bounded on a subset
N\subset M
if
\|\omega\|_{p}
is uniformly bounded for all
p\in N
.

2.4.
The bundle of Euclidean Killing fields over
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

Let
\bar{E}
denote the trivial bundle of Euclidean Killing fields over
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The identification between
\bar{E}
and
T{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times T{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
can be done along similar lines as hyperbolic case, see
[
40
, Section 3]
. Let
\bar{\kappa}\in\Gamma(\bar{E})
and
\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}
be its value at
\bar{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
We can write

\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}:=\bar{u}_{\bar{x}}+\bar{v}_{\bar{x}},

where
\bar{u}_{\bar{x}}
is the
infinitesimal pure translation at
\bar{x}
(that is,
\bar{u}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})=\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})
and
\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{u}_{\bar{x}}=0
for all
\bar{X}\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
), while
\bar{v}_{\bar{x}}
is the
infinitesimal pure rotation at
\bar{x}
(that is,
\bar{u}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})=0
and there exists a vector say
\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
such that
\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{v}_{\bar{x}}=\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})\times\bar{X}
for all
\bar{X}\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
), here
\bar{\nabla}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Therefore, we can identify a Euclidean Killing field
\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}
with a pair of tangent vectors at
\bar{x}
, say
(\bar{\tau}(\bar{x}),\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x}))\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, satisfying that

\bar{\tau}(\bar{x}):=\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x}),\quad\quad\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}=\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})\times\bar{X},

for all
\bar{X}\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

In this way, a section
\bar{\kappa}\in\Gamma(\bar{E})
is identified with a pair of vector fields
(\bar{\tau},\bar{\sigma})\in\Gamma(T{\mathbb{R}}^{3})\times\Gamma(T{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
.
we call
\bar{\tau}
(resp.
\bar{\sigma}
) the
translation
(resp.
rotation
)
component
of
\bar{\kappa}
. Under this identification and by a direct computation of the flat connection
\bar{D}
for the bundle
\bar{E}
, it follows that for any
\bar{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and any
\bar{X}\in T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, the one-form
\bar{\omega}=d^{\bar{D}}\bar{\kappa}
can be written as (see
[
40
, Section 3]
).

(6)

\bar{\omega}(\bar{X})=d^{\bar{D}}_{\bar{X}}(\bar{\tau},\bar{\sigma})=\big(\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{\tau}-\bar{\sigma}(\bar{x})\times\bar{X},\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{\sigma}\big)=:\big(\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{X}),\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{X})\big).

We call
\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\tau}}
(resp.
\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\sigma}}
) the
translation
(resp.
rotation
)
part
of
\bar{\omega}
.
Note that
\bar{\kappa}
is a constant section if and only if
d^{\bar{D}}\bar{\kappa}=0
, also, if and only if
\bar{\tau}
and
\bar{\sigma}
are Euclidean Killing fields.

2.5.
The infinitesimal Pogorelov map

We first recall the definition of the infinitesimal Pogorelov map and its key properties (see e.g.
[
31
,
38
]
and in particular
[
40
, Definitions 1.9 and 1.12]
).

Choose an affine chart
x_{0}=1
and denote by
O^{*}
the projective plane in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
dual to the center
O:=[1,0,0,0]\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
in
\mathbb{HS}^{3}
. Let
U=\mathbb{HS}^{3}\setminus(O^{*}\cup\partial\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}})
. Then
U
is the intersection of
\mathbb{HS}^{3}\setminus\partial\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
with the aforementioned affine chart of
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
. Let
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
denote the 3-dimensional Euclidean space and let
\iota:U\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be an inclusion that sends
O
to the origin
\bar{O}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, with
\iota({\mathbb{H}}^{3})
the unit open ball
\mathbb{D}^{3}
. It can be checked directly from the hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
that
\iota
is an isometry on the tangent space at
O
.

For any
x\in U
and any vector
v\in T_{x}U
, we can write
v=v^{r}+v^{\perp}
, where
v^{r}
is tangent to the geodesic in
\mathbb{HS}^{3}
going through
O
and
x
(called the
radial
component of
v
) and
v^{\perp}
is orthogonal to
v^{r}
(called the
lateral
component of
v
). Let
\Upsilon:TU\rightarrow T{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be the bundle map over the inclusion
\iota
, which is defined in the following way:

\Upsilon(v)=d\iota(v)

for all
v\in T_{O}U
, and

\Upsilon(v)=\sqrt{\frac{\langle\hat{x},\hat{x}\rangle_{HS}}{\langle d\iota(\hat{x}),d\iota(\hat{x})\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}}}d\iota(v^{r})+d\iota(v^{\perp}),

for all
v\in T_{x}U
with
x\in U
, where
\hat{x}
is a non-zero radial vector, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{HS}
is the HS scalar product. We call this bundle map an
infinitesimal Pogorelov map
.

Note that
\Upsilon
sends radial (resp. lateral) directions to radial (resp. lateral) directions. In particular, the radial component of
\Upsilon(v)
has the same norm as the radial component of
v
whenever
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
or
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
(with respect to corresponding metrics), while
\Upsilon
acts on the lateral component as the differential of the projective model map: the norm of the lateral component of
\Upsilon(v)
is
1/{\cosh(\rho)}
(resp.
1/\sinh(\rho)
) times that of the lateral component of
v
for
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
x\in{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
), where
\rho
is the hyperbolic distance from
x
to
O
(resp. the dS distance from
x
to
O^{*}
), see e.g.
[
40
, Propositions 1.2 and 1.3]
.

We say that a vector field on a surface
S\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp. a spacelike surface
S^{\prime}\subset{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
) is
isometric
if it preserves the induced metric on
S\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
S^{\prime}\subset{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
) at first order. Similarly for that on a surface
\bar{S}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. We stress the following property of the bundle map
\Upsilon
, see
[
40
, Lemma 1.10]
, which is crucial for the local rigidity problem here.

Lemma 2.2
.

The following statements are true:

(1)

Let
Z
be a vector field on
U
. Then
Z
is a Killing vector field (for the HS metric) if and only if
\Upsilon(Z)
is a Killing vector field (for the Euclidean metric).

(2)

Let
v
be a vector field on an embedded surface
S\subset U
. Then
v
is an isometric vector field on
S
if and only if
\Upsilon(v)
is an isometric vector field on
\iota(S)
.

As a consequence of Lemma
2.2
, the infinitesimal Pogorelov map
\Upsilon
induces the following map between sections of
E
(restricted to
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
) and
\bar{E}
(see e.g.
[
40
, Definition 3.5]
):

\begin{split}\Psi:E&\rightarrow\bar{E}\\
\kappa&\mapsto\bar{\kappa},\end{split}

which takes a hyperbolic Killing field
\kappa_{x}
(associated to
x
) to a Euclidean Killing field
\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}
(associated to
\bar{x}=\iota(x)
), with
\bar{\kappa}_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y}):=\Upsilon(\kappa_{x}(y))
for all
y\in{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and
\bar{y}=\iota(y)
. For convenience, we also denote by
\Psi:\Gamma(E)\rightarrow\Gamma(\bar{E})
the restriction of
\Psi
to
\Gamma(E)
. In particular,
\Psi
sends flat sections of
E
for
D
to flat sections of
\bar{E}
for
\bar{D}
and therefore, it respects the connections
D
and
\bar{D}

[
40
, Proposition 3.6]
. We have

(7)

\Psi(d^{D}_{X}\kappa):=d^{\bar{D}}_{d\iota(X)}(\Psi(\kappa)),

for any section
\kappa\in\Gamma(E)
and
X\in T{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. For simplicity, we denote
\Psi_{*}(d^{D}\kappa):=d^{\bar{D}}(\Psi(\kappa))
.

2.6.
The duality between
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}

The (polar) duality between a point (resp. a totally geodesic plane) in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and a totally geodesic plane (resp. a point) in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
is defined in the following way.

Consider the hyperboloid models
H^{3}
and
dS^{3}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
. Given a point
x\in H^{3}
(which is the intersection with
H^{3}
of the line
l_{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
in the direction of
x
), we define the
dual
of
x
as the intersection
(l_{x})^{\perp}\cap dS^{3}
, where
(l_{x})^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
of
l_{x}
with respect to the Minkowski metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{3,1}
. Given a totally geodesic plane
P\subset H^{3}
(which is obtained from the intersection with
H^{3}
of a hyperspace
H_{P}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3,1}
), we define the
dual
of
P
as the intersection
(H_{P})^{\perp}\cap dS^{3}
. Similarly, we can define the dual of a point
x^{\prime}\in dS^{3}
and the dual of a totally geodesic spacelike plane
P^{\prime}\subset dS^{3}
(note that the spacelike condition ensures that the intersection
(H_{P^{\prime}})^{\perp}\cap H^{3}
is non-empty and the dual of
P^{\prime}
is thus well-defined). Through the projective maps, the duality is naturally defined in the projective models
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.

Using this duality, one can define the
dual surface
, denoted by
S^{*}
, of a convex embedded surface
S\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
as the set of points which are dual to the tangent planes
P_{x}
to
S
at all
x\in S
. Similarly, one can define the
dual surface
, denoted by
(S^{\prime})^{*}
, of a convex embedded spacelike surface
S^{\prime}\subset{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
. In particular, we denote by
x^{*}
the point in
S^{*}
which is dual to the tangent plane to
S
at
x\in S
.

We list the following relations and properties between dual surfaces, see e.g.
[
38
, Section 3]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
S\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
be a strictly convex embedded surface. Then

(1)

The dual surface
S^{*}
of
S
is a strictly convex space-like surface in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.

(2)

The dual surface
(S^{*})^{*}
of
S^{*}
is exactly
S
.

(3)

The pull-back of the induced metric on
S^{*}
through the duality map is the third fundamental form of
S
and vice versa.

(4)

S
has curvature
K\in(-1,+\infty)
at a point
x\in S
if and only if
S^{*}
has curvature
K^{*}=K/(K+1)\in(-\infty,1)
at the dual point
x^{*}\in S^{*}
.

3.
Local rigidity

3.1.
General overview

Let
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
be a convex co-compact hyperbolic structure on
M
with conformal structure at infinity
c_{i}
on
\partial_{i}M
(for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
), with induced metric
h_{j}
on a smooth, strictly convex surface
S_{j}\subset M
isotopic to
\partial_{j}M
(for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
), and with third fundamental form
h^{*}_{k}
on a smooth, strictly convex surface
S_{k}\subset M
isotopic to
\partial_{k}M
(for each
k\in\mathcal{K}
). In this section, we aim to show the infinitesimal rigidity of
g
with respect to
c_{i}
,
h_{j}
and
h^{*}_{k}
over
i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}
(as stated below).

Proposition 3.1
.

For any first-order deformation
\dot{g}\in T_{g}{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
, if
\dot{g}
preserves the conformal structure at infinity
c_{i}
on
\partial_{i}M
(for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
), the induced metric
h_{j}
on
S_{j}
(for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
) and the third fundamental form
h^{*}_{k}
on
S_{k}
(for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
) at first order, then
\dot{g}
is trivial.

3.1.1.
From
M
to
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}

For simplicity, we denote by
M
the hyperbolic manifold with convex co-compact structure
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
. Let
N\subset M
be the geodesically convex subset with the conformal boundary at infinity
\partial_{i}M
and the smooth, strictly convex boundary surfaces
S_{j}
and
S_{k}
over
i\in\mathcal{I}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
k\in\mathcal{K}
. We denote
\partial_{i}N:=\partial_{i}M
,
\partial_{j}N:=S_{j}
and
\partial_{k}N:=S_{k}
.

Let
\Omega
denote the the lift of
N
in the universal cover of
M
(which is identified with
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
). Up to isometries, we always assume that the center
O
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is contained in the interior of the convex hull of the limit set
\Lambda
of the Klein group of
M
(which descends to the convex core
C_{M}
of
M
), and is thus contained in the interior of
\Omega
. Moreover, we assume that the family
(dev_{t})_{t\in[0,\epsilon]}
of developing maps associated to the first order deformation
\dot{g}
(see Section
2.3.1
) is normalized such that the deformation section vanishes at
O
(namely,
\tau(O)=\sigma(O)=0
). This will help to simplify some estimates we need later (see Lemma
3.18
for instance).

We denote by
\partial_{i}\Omega\subset\partial\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
) the union of all the lifts in
\partial\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}
of the conformal boundary component
\partial_{i}M
, by
\partial_{j}\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(
j\in\mathcal{J}
) the union of all the lifts in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of
S_{j}
, and by
\partial_{k}\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(
k\in\mathcal{K}
) the union of all the lifts in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of
S_{k}
. Let
(\partial_{k}\Omega)^{*}
denote the dual surface in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
of the strictly convex surface
\partial_{k}\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
and let
\Omega^{*}
denote the convex domain in
{\mathbb{RP}}^{3}
, sharing the same boundary with
\Omega
, except with
\partial_{k}\Omega
replaced by
(\partial_{k}\Omega)^{*}
.

3.1.2.
From
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

Recall that
\iota
is an inclusion in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
that sends the projective model
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
to the unit open ball
\mathbb{D}^{3}
(see Section
2.5
).
We denote
\bar{\Omega}:=\iota(\Omega)
,
\partial_{l}\bar{\Omega}=\iota(\partial_{l}\Omega)
(
1\leq l\leq n
)
and
\bar{\Lambda}=\iota(\Lambda)
. Note that the center
\bar{O}
of
\mathbb{D}^{3}
is contained in the interior of
\bar{\Omega}
(by the above assumption) and

\partial\bar{\Omega}=(\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega})\cup(\cup_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\partial_{j}\bar{\Omega})\cup(\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega})\cup\bar{\Lambda}.

Let
\bar{\Omega}^{*}=\iota(\Omega^{*})
and denote
\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}^{*}:=\iota((\partial_{k}\Omega)^{*})
,
k\in\mathcal{K}
. Then

\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}=(\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega})\cup(\cup_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\partial_{j}\bar{\Omega})\cup(\cup_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}^{*})\cup\bar{\Lambda}.

In the case that
M
is quasifuchsian, the limit set
\bar{\Lambda}
is a Jordan curve and
\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
has two connected components. In the case that
M
has
n>2
boundary components,

\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
is a countably infinite union of disjoint open disks, and for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
(resp.
j\in\mathcal{J}
,
k\in\mathcal{K}
),
\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial_{j}\bar{\Omega}
,
\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}
and
\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) has countably infinitely many connected components.

3.1.3.
The boundary behavior of the convex domains
\bar{\Omega}
and
\bar{\Omega}^{*}
.

The following describes the behavior of the principal curvatures of the image in
{\mathbb{D}}^{3}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(resp.
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}^{3}}
) under
\iota
of a strictly convex surface in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp. a strictly convex spacelike surface in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
), based on the fact that the second fundamental form of the surface changes conformally (see
[
40
, Lemmas 1.4 and 2.8]
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
S
be a smooth surface in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
or a spacelike smooth surface in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
. If
S
has principal curvatures in
[k_{min},k_{max}]
for some
k_{max}>k_{min}>0
. Then
\iota(S)
is a convex surface in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, with principal curvatures in
[k^{\prime}_{min},k^{\prime}_{max}]
for some
k^{\prime}_{max}>k^{\prime}_{min}>0
.

Remark 3.3
.

It is shown by Sullivan
[
44
]
that
\bar{\Lambda}
has Hausdorff dimension in
[1,2)
. Moreover, Bishop has proved that the Hausdorff dimension and Minkowski dimension are the same for the limit set of analytically finite Klein groups
[
9
]
.

Remark 3.4
.

The surface
\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
(resp.

\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
) is smooth, while
\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) is
C^{1,1}
(see e.g.
[
40
, Corollaries 2.6 and 2.9]
). In particular, each connected component of
\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial_{j}\bar{\Omega}
,
\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}
,
\partial_{k}\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) is contained in
\partial{\mathbb{D}}^{3}
(resp. tangent to
\partial{\mathbb{D}}^{3}
along its boundary).

Note that we do not claim that
\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) is
C^{2}
, however the principal curvatures are well-defined almost everywhere and we do claim that they are bounded:

Lemma 3.5
.

\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) is in the class
D_{2,\infty}
, namely, it satisfies the following:

(1)

The principal curvatures on
\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) has positive lower and upper bounds almost everywhere.

(2)

\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
) can be locally written as the graph of a function with bounded measurable second derivatives.

Proof.

Statement (1) follows from Lemma
3.2
, Remarks
3.3
and
3.4
, and the fact that
\partial_{i}\Omega
(resp.
\partial_{j}\Omega
,
\partial_{k}\Omega
and
(\partial_{k}\Omega)^{*}
) descends to a closed, strictly convex surface in
M
, with principal curvatures bounded between two positive constants. Statement (2) follows from Statement (1), the
C^{1,1}
-smoothness (see Remark
3.4
) and strictly convexity of
\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
).
∎

For each
\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
(resp.
\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
), let
\theta_{\bar{x}}
denote the angle between the radial direction along
\bar{O}\bar{x}
at
\bar{x}
and the outward-pointing normal direction to
\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
) at
\bar{x}
and let
\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})
(resp.
\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})
) denote the distance from the point
\bar{x}
to the limit set
\bar{\Lambda}
along
\partial\bar{\Omega}
(resp.
\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}
).

Since
\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega}\subset\partial{\mathbb{D}}^{3}
, it is clear that
\theta_{\bar{x}}=0
for all
\bar{x}\in\partial_{i}\bar{\Omega}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
). For the other

\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}\cap{\mathbb{D}}^{3}
or
\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}^{3}}
, the estimate of the angle
\theta_{\bar{x}}
and the distances
\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})
,
\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})
in
[
40
, Lemmas 2.4 and 2.7]
still work and we conclude the following:

Lemma 3.6
.

There exists a constant
C>1
, such that

(1)

For any
\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}\cap{\mathbb{D}}^{3}

with
\bar{x}
close enough to
\bar{\Lambda}
, we have
\theta_{\bar{x}}\leq C\sqrt{1-\|\bar{x}\|}
and moreover,

\frac{\sqrt{1-\|\bar{x}\|}}{C}\leq\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})\leq C\sqrt{1-\|\bar{x}\|}.

(2)

For any
\bar{x}\in\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}\setminus\overline{{\mathbb{D}}^{3}}
with
\bar{x}
close enough to
\bar{\Lambda}
, we have

\theta_{\bar{x}}\leq C\sqrt{\|\bar{x}\|-1}
and moreover,

\frac{\sqrt{\|\bar{x}\|-1}}{C}\leq\delta_{\partial\bar{\Omega}^{*}}(\bar{x},\bar{\Lambda})\leq C\sqrt{\|\bar{x}\|-1}.

Remark 3.7
.

Let
\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}
be the lift in
\Omega
of the equidistant surface
S^{i}_{t}
(which is a closed strictly convex surface) at distance
t
from
\partial_{i}C_{M}
(see Subsection
3.2.2
) and let
\bar{S}^{i}_{t}:=\iota(\tilde{S}^{i}_{t})
. Note that for each
t\geq 1
, the principal curvatures of
\bar{S}^{i}_{t}
have a uniform positive lower and upper bound for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
(by Lemma
3.2
and Remark
3.15
). Statement (1) in Lemma
3.6
still holds for any
\bar{x}\in\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\bar{S}^{i}_{t}
(with
\partial\bar{\Omega}
replaced by
\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\bar{S}^{i}_{t}
) close enough to
\bar{\Lambda}
.

3.1.4.
The Vekua lemma

Let
\bar{I}
(resp.
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}
,
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}
) and
\bar{B}
denote the first (resp. second, third) fundamental form and the shape operator of an embedded strictly convex surface
\bar{S}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. We define

\bar{B}(\bar{X}):=-\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{X}}\bar{n},\quad\quad\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=\bar{I}(\bar{B}(\bar{X}),\bar{Y}),\quad\quad\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=\bar{I}(\bar{B}(\bar{X}),\bar{B}(\bar{Y})),

for
\bar{X},\bar{Y}\in T\bar{S}
, where
\bar{n}
is the unit normal vector field on
\bar{S}
oriented such that the eigenvalues of
\bar{B}
are positive, and
\bar{\nabla}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

Let
\bar{J}
be the complex structure associated to
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}
and let
\nabla^{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}
denote the Levi-Civita connection for
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}
. By computation (see
[
41
, Proposition 3.1]
),

\nabla^{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{Y}=\bar{B}^{-1}\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}(\bar{B}\bar{Y}),

where
\bar{X},\bar{Y}\in T\bar{S}
and
\nabla^{\bar{I}}
is the Levi-Civita connection for
\bar{I}
.

Let
\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}
be the operator that takes a vector field
\bar{W}
over
\bar{S}
to a section
\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}\bar{W}
of the bundle of one-forms with values in
T\bar{S}
, defined by

(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}\bar{W})(\bar{X}):={\nabla}^{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{W}+\bar{J}{\nabla}^{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}_{\bar{J}\bar{X}}\bar{W},

for all
\bar{X}\in T\bar{S}
. It follows from a direct computation that

(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}\bar{W})(\bar{J}\bar{X})=-\bar{J}(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}\bar{W})(\bar{X})~.

Let
\bar{v}
be a vector field on
\bar{S}
. The following equation will play a central role, so we give it a specific name.

(E)

\bar{\partial}_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}(\bar{B}^{-1}\bar{v})=0~.

It ensures that the Lie derivative of the induced metric
\bar{I}
on
\bar{S}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with respect to
\bar{v}
is parallel to
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}
(see the proof of Lemma
3.8
below for more details).

The following states a crucial relation between an isometric deformation vector field on an embedded surface
\bar{S}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and equation (
E
).

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\bar{U}
be an isometric deformation vector field on an embedded convex surface
\bar{S}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(with the shape operator
\bar{B}
) and let
\bar{V}
be its component tangent to
\bar{S}
. Then
\bar{V}
satisfies equation
(E)
. Conversely, let
\bar{V}
be a solution of equation (
E
), there exists a unique isometric deformation vector field of
\bar{S}
whose component tangent to
\bar{S}
is
\bar{V}
.

Proof.

The first statement was shown in
[
41
, Proposition 3.8]
– note that we refer here to the arxiv version (v4) rather than to the published version.

It suffices to show the second statement. Assume that
\bar{V}
is a solution of equation (
E
), then by a direct computation (see also
[
41
, Proposition 3.8]
), we have

(8)

\begin{split}\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\partial_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}(\bar{B}^{-1}\bar{V})\bar{X},\bar{X})=\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{X})-\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{J}\bar{X})=0,\\
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\partial_{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}}(\bar{B}^{-1}\bar{V})\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X})=\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{J}\bar{X})+\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{X})=0.\\
\end{split}

Since
\bar{J}
is the complex structure associated to
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}
, then
\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X})=0
. Note that

(L_{\bar{V}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{Y})+\bar{I}(\bar{X},\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{Y}}\bar{V}).

Assume that
\bar{X}\not=0
. When
\bar{Y}=\bar{J}\bar{X}
, combined with (
8
), then

(L_{\bar{V}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X})=0=\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X})=2\frac{\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{X})}{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{X})}\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X}).

When
\bar{Y}=\bar{X}
, using (
8
) again, then

(L_{\bar{V}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{X})=2\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{X})=2\frac{\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{X}}\bar{V},\bar{X})}{\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{X})}\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{X}).

Define
f(\bar{x}):=\bar{I}(\nabla^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}}}\bar{V},\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}})/\bar{I}\hskip-2.84544ptI(\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}},\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}})
, where
\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}}\in T_{\bar{x}}\bar{S}
is an arbitrary non-zero vector. By (
8
) and the fact that
\bar{I}\hskip-2.84544ptI(\bar{X},\bar{X})=\bar{I}\hskip-2.84544ptI(\bar{J}\bar{X},\bar{J}\bar{X})
, it can be directly checked that
f
depends only on the base point of
\bar{Z}_{\bar{x}}
and is thus a well-defined function on
\bar{S}
. Therefore,

(9)

(L_{\bar{V}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=2f\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{Y}).

Let
\bar{U}:=\bar{V}+f\bar{n}
, where
\bar{n}
is the unit normal vector field on
\bar{S}
defined above. Then by (
9
),

(L_{\bar{U}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=(L_{\bar{V}+f\bar{n}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=(L_{\bar{V}}\bar{I})(\bar{X},\bar{Y})-2f\bar{I\hskip-2.84544ptI}(\bar{X},\bar{Y})=0.

As a consequence,
\bar{V}
is the tangent component of the unique isometric vector field
\bar{U}
on
\bar{S}
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Note that in Lemma
3.8
, the surface
\bar{S}
can be locally written as the graph of a function say
u=f(x,y)
. If
\bar{U}
is an isometric deformation vector field on
\bar{S}
, then the tangent component
\bar{V}
of
\bar{U}
satisfies the generalized Beltrami equation:

(10)

w_{\bar{z}}-\mu(z)(w_{z}+\bar{w}_{\bar{z}})=0,

where
w(z)
is the local expression of the tangent component
\bar{V}
in a local chart
z=x+iy
, and
\mu(z)=(1/2)(f_{\bar{z}\bar{z}}/f_{z\bar{z}})
with
\|\mu\|_{\infty}<1
(see e.g.
[
47
, V.2.2, Eq (2.9) p.397]
). By Lemma
3.8
, the tangent component
\bar{V}
satisfies equation (
E
) if and only if it satisfies equation (
10
).

A solution of equation (
E
) is said to be
trivial
if it is the tangent component (restricted to
\bar{S}
) of a global Killing field of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, which corresponds to a trivial infinitesimal deformation of
\bar{S}
(see
[
41
, Section 5]
and
[
47
, V.1, p. 394]
).

Definition 3.10
.

Assume that a vector field
\bar{v}
on
\bar{S}
has the expression
\bar{v}=f(z)\partial_{z}
in local conformal coordinates
z=x+iy
. We say
\bar{v}
is in the class
D_{1,p}(T\bar{S})
(where
p\geq 1
) if the generalized derivative of
f
is in
L^{p}(\bar{S})
, in terms of the integration of the product of
f
and the derivative (with respect to
z
or
\bar{z}
) of any
C^{1}
function with compact support.

We now introduce a result of Vekua
[
47
, V.2.2, p. 393-402]
, which is crucial for the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Lemma 3.11

(Vekua)
.

Let
\bar{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be a bounded convex subset such that
\partial\bar{\Omega}
is in
D_{2,\infty}
. Suppose that
\bar{v}
is a solution of (
E
) which is in
D_{1,p}(T\partial\bar{\Omega})
. Then
\bar{v}
is trivial.

We will use this lemma through
[
40
, Lemma 5.5]
, which we recall here in slightly different notations.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\bar{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
be a bounded convex subset. Let
\bar{\Lambda}\subset\partial\bar{\Omega}
be a closed subset of Hausdorff dimension
D_{\bar{\Lambda}}\in(1,2)
. Let
p>2
and let
f:\partial\bar{\Omega}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a function such that

•

f
is everywhere
C^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(D_{\bar{\Lambda}}-1,1)
.

•

f
is smooth outside
\bar{\Lambda}
.

•

\int_{\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}}\|df\|^{p}da<\infty
,
where
da
is the area form of the induced metric on
\partial\bar{\Omega}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

Then
df
, considered as an
L^{p}
section (defined on
\partial\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Lambda}
as the usual derivative of
f
, and as
0
on
\bar{\Lambda}
), is the generalized derivative of
f
on
\partial\bar{\Omega}
, and
f
is in
D_{1,p}(T\partial\bar{\Omega})
.

3.2.
Estimates on
\omega

In this subsection, we aim to show the following statement:

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\dot{g}
be a first-order deformation of the hyperbolic structure
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
which leaves invariant the conformal metric at infinity
c_{i}
on
\partial_{i}M=\partial_{i}N
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
, the induced metric
h_{j}
on
S_{j}=\partial_{j}N
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and the third fundamental form
h_{k}
on
S_{k}=\partial_{k}N
,
k\in\mathcal{K}
, at first order. There exists a representative
\omega
of
\dot{g}
, which is a closed one-form in
\Omega^{1}(M,E_{M})
such that:

(i)

\omega
vanishes along the geodesic rays starting orthogonally from a support plane to
\partial C_{M}
, at all points at distance at least
1
from
C_{M}
.

(ii)

for each
x\in N
,
\|\omega\|_{x}\leq C\exp(-2d(x,C_{M}))
for a constant
C>0
depending on
\omega
.

3.2.1.
The deformation one-form on
\partial_{i}M

Let
\sigma_{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
) denote the complex projective structure on
\partial_{i}M
. By assumption, the first-order deformation
\dot{g}
of
M
preserves the conformal structure at infinity on
\partial_{i}M
, so the deformation vector field
u
restricted to
\partial_{i}\Omega
, say
u^{i}
, is holomorphic and has the form

u^{i}(z)=f_{i}(z)\partial_{z}~,

where
f_{i}(z)
is a holomorphic function in an affine chart of
\sigma_{i}
. The (infinitesimal) Schwarzian derivative of
u^{i}
has the following expression (see e.g.
[
13
, Section 2]
):

\mathcal{S}(u^{i})(z)=f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z)dz^{2}~.

One can check that
\mathcal{S}(u^{i})(\gamma\cdot z)=\mathcal{S}(u^{i})(z)
for all
\gamma\in PSL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, so it determines a quadratic differential on
\partial_{i}M
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(u^{i})\equiv 0
if and only if
f_{i}(z)
is a quadratic polynomial.

Note that the canonical lift
s_{u^{i}}
of
u^{i}
has local expression in the form of (
5
). Define
\omega^{i}:=d^{D}s_{u^{i}}
. Then for each
z_{0}\in\partial_{i}\Omega
and
U=u(z)\partial_{z}\in T_{z_{0}}{\mathbb{C}}
,

(11)

\begin{split}\omega^{i}(U)(z_{0})=(d^{D}_{U}s_{u^{i}})(z_{0})&=\big(u(z)\partial_{z}\big)|_{z_{0}}\big((s_{u^{i}})(z)\big)\\
&=u(z_{0})\partial_{z_{0}}\big(f_{i}(z_{0})+f^{\prime}_{i}(z_{0})(z-z_{0})+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})}{2}(z-z_{0})^{2}\big)\partial_{z}\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{2}u(z_{0})f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})(z-z_{0})^{2}\right)\partial_{z}~.\end{split}

As a result,
(d^{D}{s_{u^{i}}})
takes values in the vector space of homogeneous polynomials of degree 2 (see also
[
30
, Lemma 4.8]
).

3.2.2.
The equidistant foliations of hyperbolic ends

It is well-known that
M\setminus C_{M}
has
n
connected components, each called a
hyperbolic end
of
M
, denoted by
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
(
1\leq l\leq n
). The connected component of the boundary of
C_{M}
as a pleated boundary surface of
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
is denoted by
\partial_{l}C_{M}
. Moreover, each
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
is foliated by a family of equidistant surfaces
(S^{l}_{t})_{t>0}
lying at a distance
t
from
S^{l}_{0}:=\partial_{l}C_{M}
. Since
\partial C_{M}
is convex, for each point
p\in{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
, there is a unique geodesic ray (say
r_{q}
) in
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
starting from
q\in\partial_{l}C_{M}
and orthogonal to a support plane to
\partial_{l}C_{M}
at
q
and going through
p
. We define
G:M\setminus C_{M}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}M
to be the hyperbolic Gauss map that takes each point
p\in{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
to the endpoint at infinity of the geodesic ray
r_{q}
. In particular, the union
\cup_{q\in\partial_{l}C_{M}}r_{q}
of these “vertical” geodesic rays forms another foliation of
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
.

Let
\tilde{C}_{M}
(resp.
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{l}
,
\tilde{S}^{l}_{t}
) denote the lift in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of
C_{M}
(resp.
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
,
S^{l}_{t}
). Let
\tilde{G}:{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\setminus\tilde{C}_{M}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus\Lambda
be the lifting map of the Gauss map
G
.

3.2.3.
The metric at infinity associated to an equidistant foliation of hyperbolic ends

There are several ways to define a conformal metric at infinity on the boundary
\partial_{l}M
of a hyperbolic end
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
, by using the equidistant foliation of
{\mathcal{E}}^{l}
(see e.g.
[
30
, Section 3, 4]
). Here we consider the metric at infinity on
\partial_{i}M
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
), defined as

(12)

g_{i,\infty}:=4e^{-2t}G_{*}I^{*}_{t}~,

where
I^{*}_{t}=I_{t}+2I\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}+I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}
is the “horospherical metric” on an equidistant surface
S^{i}_{t}
(
t\geq 1
), with first, second and third fundamental forms on
S^{i}_{t}
denoted by
I_{t},I\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t},I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}
.
We stress that the metric
g_{i,\infty}
is independent of the choice of
t
, and moreover, its conformal structure is the same as that underlies the
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
structure on
\partial_{i}M
(see
[
39
]
and
[
10
, Section 3.3]
for more details). (This metric
g_{i,\infty}
is then the Thurston metric at infinity.)

Let
x_{t}\in\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}
(
t\geq 1
). Let
z_{0}=\tilde{G}(x_{t})
and
z_{1}
be the other endpoint at infinity of the complete geodesic passing through
x_{t}
and going towards
z_{0}
on one side. We choose an affine chart
z
near
z_{0}
such that
z_{0}
is identified with
0
, while
z_{1}
is identified with
\infty
in the Poincaré upper half-space model
{\mathbb{U}}^{3}:=\{(z,s):z\in{\mathbb{C}},s>0\}
, and moreover,
the metric
\tilde{g}_{i,\infty}
, as the lift of
g_{i,\infty}
to
\partial_{i}\Omega
, has the following expression on
T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}\Omega)\subset T_{z_{0}}{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
:

(13)

\tilde{g}_{i,\infty}:=4e^{-2t}\tilde{G}_{*}\tilde{I}^{*}_{t}=|dz|^{2},

where
\tilde{I}^{*}_{t}
is the lifting metric on
\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}
of
I^{*}_{t}
.

Note that the deformation one form on
\partial_{i}\Omega
takes values in the projective fields given by homogeneous polynomials of degree 2, see (
11
). We now consider the extension of such projective fields to Killing fields in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, which will be used later in the proof of Lemma
3.13
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
x_{t}\in\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}
(
t\geq 1
) and let
z
be an affine chart near
z_{0}
as chosen above. Let
P(z)=\lambda(z-z_{0})^{2}\partial_{z}
(
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
\lambda\not=0
) be a projective vector field on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus\{z_{1}\}
. Then the norm of the Killing field extending
P(z)
, at
x_{t}
, is
4|\lambda|e^{-t}
.
In particular, the norm decreases to zero as
x_{t}
tends to
z_{0}
along the geodesic ray
r_{x_{0}}
orthogonal to
\tilde{S}^{i}_{0}
at
x_{0}
.

Proof.

We consider the model
{\mathbb{U}}^{3}:=\{(z,s):z\in{\mathbb{C}},~s>0\}
, with the metric
(|dz|^{2}+ds^{2})/s^{2}
. Let
\big(\phi_{t}(z)\big)_{t\in[0,\epsilon]}
be a family of Möbius transformations generating
P(z)
, with
\phi_{0}(z)=\rm{Id}
and

\phi_{t}(z):=\frac{a(t)z+b(t)}{c(t)z+d(t)},

where
a(t),b(t),c(t),d(t)\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
a(t)d(t)-b(t)c(t)=1
.
A computation shows that

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|_{t=0}\phi_{t}(z)=-c^{\prime}(0)z^{2}+(a^{\prime}(0)-d^{\prime}(0))z+b^{\prime}(0).

Therefore,

(14)

-c^{\prime}(0)=\lambda,\quad a^{\prime}(0)-d^{\prime}(0)=b^{\prime}(0)=0.

Moreover,
a(t)d(t)-b(t)c(t)\equiv 1
. Differentiating on both sides of this identity, we obtain
a^{\prime}(0)+d^{\prime}(0)=0
. Combined with (
14
), we have that
a^{\prime}(0)=d^{\prime}(0)=0
.

Now we construct a Killing field on
{\mathbb{U}}^{3}
whose extension to
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus\{z_{1}\}
is
P(z)
. Note that if
c(t)\not=0
, there is a canonical way to extend
\phi_{t}(z)
to an isometry on
\mathbb{U}^{3}
(see
[
28
, Chapter 1.1]
) by

(15)

\tilde{\phi}_{t}(z,s):=\Big(-\frac{\overline{z+\big(d(t)/c(t)\big)}}{c^{2}(t)\big(|z+(d(t)/c(t))|^{2}+s^{2}\big)}+\frac{a(t)}{c(t)},\,\,\frac{s}{|c(t)|^{2}\big(|z+\big(d(t)/c(t)\big)|^{2}+s^{2}\big)}\Big).

Taking the derivative of (
15
) at
t=0
, the desired Killing field, say
\kappa
, on
\mathbb{U}^{3}
is

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|_{t=0}\tilde{\phi}_{t}(z,s)=\big(\lambda z^{2}-\overline{\lambda}s^{2},\,\,2s\cdot{\rm Re}(\lambda z)\big).

This is a parabolic Killing field that vanishes at
0
and preserves (globally) the horospheres centered at
0
. Using the orthonormal frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}):=(s\partial_{x},s\partial_{y},s\partial_{s})
of
T\mathbb{U}^{3}
and by a direct computation, we have

\begin{split}\nabla_{e_{1}}e_{1}&=e_{3},\quad\nabla_{e_{2}}e_{1}=0,\quad\nabla_{e_{3}}e_{1}=0,\\
\nabla_{e_{1}}e_{2}&=0,\quad\nabla_{e_{2}}e_{2}=e_{3},\quad\nabla_{e_{3}}e_{2}=0,\\
\nabla_{e_{1}}e_{3}&=-e_{1},\quad\nabla_{e_{2}}e_{3}=-e_{2},\quad\nabla_{e_{3}}e_{3}=0.\\
\end{split}

Combined with the formula (
1
), we obtain

\frac{1}{2}{\rm curl}(\kappa)=\big(-i\lambda z^{2}-i\overline{\lambda}s^{2},\,\,2s\cdot{\rm Im}(\lambda z)\big).

Thus the norm of
\kappa
at the point
x_{t}=(0,s(x_{t}))
is

\|\kappa\|_{x_{t}}=\|\tau(x_{t})\|_{\mathbb{U}^{3}}+\|\sigma(x_{t})\|_{\mathbb{U}^{3}}=\|(-\overline{\lambda}s^{2}(x_{t}),0)\|_{\mathbb{U}^{3}}+\|((-i\overline{\lambda}s^{2}(x_{t}),0)\|_{\mathbb{U}^{3}}=2|\lambda|s(x_{t}),

which decreases to zero as
x_{t}
tends to
z_{0}
along the geodesic ray orthogonal to
\tilde{S}^{i}_{0}
at
x_{0}
(note that
s(x_{t})
tends to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
).

By (
13
), under the chosen affine chart
z
near
z_{0}
,

\tilde{G}_{*}(\tilde{I}^{*}_{t}|_{T_{x_{t}}\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}})=\frac{1}{4}(e^{2t}|dz|^{2})|_{T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}\Omega)}.

Note that the coordinate
s(x_{t})
is uniquely determined by the above choice of affine chart
z
. Since the horospherical metric
\tilde{I}_{t}^{*}
on
T_{x_{t}}\tilde{S}^{i}_{t}
is equal to the metric on the tangent space of the horosphere
\{s=s(x_{t})\}
at
x_{t}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{4}e^{2t}|dz|^{2}=\frac{1}{s(x_{t})^{2}}|dz|^{2}~.

It follows that
s(x_{t})=2e^{-t}
.

As a consequence, the norm of the Killing field extending
P(z)
, at
x_{t}
, is equal to
4|\lambda|e^{-t}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Let
G_{t}:S^{i}_{t}\rightarrow\partial_{i}M
(
t\geq 1
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
) denote the hyperbolic Gauss map. Since
S^{i}_{1}
is strictly convex, by a direct computation, the principal curvatures
\lambda_{t}(p)
,
\mu_{t}(p)
of the equidistant surface
S^{i}_{t}
(
t\geq 1
) are respectively

\lambda_{t}(p)=\frac{\lambda_{1}(p)+\tanh t}{1+\lambda_{1}(p)\tanh t},\quad\quad\mu_{t}(p)=\frac{\mu_{1}(p)+\tanh t}{1+\mu_{1}(p)\tanh t}~.

Therefore, the principal curvatures of the closed surface
S^{i}_{t}
have a uniform positive upper and lower bound (and thus the second and third fundamental forms
I\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}
and
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}
are uniformly bounded) for all
t\geq 1
, say
k^{i}_{\rm max}
and
k^{i}_{\rm min}
. Recall that the horospherical metric on
S^{i}_{t}
is
I^{*}_{t}=I_{t}+2I\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}+I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{t}
. As a result, on
T_{p}S^{i}_{t}
,

I_{t}\leq I_{t}^{*}\leq(1+k)^{2}I_{t},

where
k=\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\{k^{i}_{\rm max}\}
.

3.2.4.
Proof of Lemma
3.13
.

We are now ready to give the proof.

Proof of Lemma
3.13
.

Let
(g_{t})_{t\in[0,\varepsilon]}
be a one-parameter deformation
of
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
with derivative at
t=0
corresponding to
\dot{g}
(up to a trivial deformation). We now construct the desired representative one-form
\omega
by the following steps:

Step
1

Let
u_{0}
be a deformation (automorphic) vector field on
\Omega
associated to
\dot{g}
,
whose restriction to
\partial_{i}\Omega
is a holomorphic (automorphic) vector field, denoted by
u^{i}_{0}
. Let
s_{0}
denote the (automorphic) deformation section associated to
u_{0}
and in particular, let
s^{i}_{0}
denote the deformation section associated to
u_{0}^{i}
(see Section
2.3
). For each point
y\in\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
), there is a unique point
x\in\tilde{S}^{i}_{0}
such that
y
lies in the geodesic ray
r_{x}
orthogonal to a support plane to
\tilde{S}^{i}_{0}
at
x
. Let
z\in\partial_{i}\Omega
be the endpoint at infinity of
r_{x}
and let
(s^{i}_{0})_{z}
be the projective vector field on
\partial_{i}\Omega
, which is the value in
sl_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
at
z
of
s^{i}_{0}
. There is a unique Killing field on
\Omega
, denoted by
\kappa^{i}_{x}
, whose extension to
\partial_{i}\Omega
is
(s^{i}_{0})_{z}
. For each
y\in r_{x}
, we associate to
y
the same Killing field
\kappa^{i}_{x}
. Then we obtain a section
\kappa
over
\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}
, which is constant along
r_{x}
for each
x\in\tilde{S}^{i}_{0}
. Besides,
\kappa
is automorphic, since each section
s^{i}_{0}
is automorphic.

Step
2

Let
\eta(t)
be a smooth cut-off function which vanishes for
t\leq\delta_{0}
(where
\delta_{0}<1
is taken small enough) and is equal to 1 for
t\geq 1
.
Let
\chi_{E}
be the characteristic function of
E\subset\Omega
, which is equal to
1
on
E
and to
0
on the complement of
E
. Now we construct a new section, say
s
, over
\Omega
:

s:=\big(1-\eta\circ d(\cdot,C_{M})\cdot\chi_{\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}}\big)s_{0}+\big(\eta\circ d(\cdot,C_{M})\cdot\chi_{\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}}\big)\kappa.

Then
s
coincides with
\kappa
near
\partial_{i}\Omega
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
and coincides with
s_{0}
near the convex subset
\tilde{C}_{M}
. It is clear that
s
is automorphic.

Step
3

Let
\tilde{\omega}=d^{D}s
. Then
\tilde{\omega}
is a one-form on
\Omega
, which descends to a closed one-form say
\omega
, on
N
, which is cohomologous to the one-form, say
\omega_{0}
, that descends from
\tilde{\omega}_{0}:=d^{D}s_{0}
. Indeed,

\tilde{\omega}-\tilde{\omega}_{0}=d^{D}(s-s_{0})=\big(-\eta\circ d(\cdot,C_{M})\cdot\chi_{\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}}\big)d^{D}(s_{0}-\kappa),

where
d^{D}(s_{0}-\kappa)
is exact on
\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}^{i}
.

By construction,
\omega
vanishes along each geodesic ray starting from
S^{i}_{1}
orthogonally for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, which implies Condition (i). It remains to show
\omega
also satisfies Condition (ii).

We first estimate the norm of
\omega
on the subset
\cup_{t\geq 1}S^{i}_{t}
of
{\mathcal{E}}^{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
). Let
p_{t}\in S^{i}_{t}
and
X\in T_{p_{t}}M
. By Condition (i), it suffices to consider
\omega(X)
for
X\in T_{p_{t}}S^{i}_{t}
. Denote
z_{0}:=G(p_{t})\in\partial_{i}M
and
U:=G_{*}X\in T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}M)
. By (
12
), the horospherical metric
I^{*}_{t}
satisfies

G_{*}(I^{*}_{t}|_{T_{p_{t}}S^{i}_{t}}):=\frac{1}{4}e^{2t}g_{i,\infty}|_{T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}M)}~.

We now choose an affine chart
z
near
z_{0}
such that
g_{i,\infty}|_{T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}M)}=|dz|^{2}
, as in (
13
).
In particular,

(16)

\|U\|_{g_{i,\infty}}=\|G_{*}X\|_{g_{i,\infty}}=\|X\|_{G^{*}g_{i,\infty}}=2e^{-t}\|X\|_{I^{*}_{t}}.

Assume that
U=u(z)\partial_{z}
in the above chosen affine chart
z
and the holomorphic deformation vector field
u_{0}^{i}
on
\partial_{i}M
is expressed as
u_{0}^{i}(z)=f_{i}(z)\partial_{z}
, where
f_{i}(z)
is a holomorphic function. It is clear that
\omega=\omega_{0}
on
\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\partial_{i}M
. Then by (
11
),

\omega(U)=\omega_{0}(U)=d^{D}_{U}s_{0}=\frac{1}{2}u(z_{0})f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})(z-z_{0})^{2}\partial_{z}.

Note that
\omega(X)
is the Killing field extending the projective field
\omega(U)
. By Lemma
3.14
, we have

\begin{split}\|\omega(X)\|_{p_{t}}&=4e^{-t}\cdot|u(z_{0})|\cdot\frac{1}{2}|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})|\\
&=2e^{-t}\cdot\|U\|_{g_{i,\infty}}\cdot|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})|\\
&=4e^{-2t}\cdot\|X\|_{I^{*}_{t}}\cdot|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})|\\
&\leq 4C_{0}e^{-2t}\cdot\|X\|_{I_{t}}\cdot|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z_{0})|\\
&\leq C_{1}e^{-2t}\cdot\|X\|_{I_{t}}.\end{split}

The second equality follows from the fact that
g_{i,\infty}|_{T_{z_{0}}(\partial_{i}M)}=|dz|^{2}
, the third one follows from (
16
), while the first inequality follows from Remark
3.15
, where
C_{0}=1+k
. Since
f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z)dz^{2}
is a quadratic differential on
\partial_{i}\Omega
and under the above affine chart (see Section
3.2.1
),
|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z)|
descends to a well-defined smooth function on the closed surface
\partial_{i}M
and is thus bounded. The last inequality follows by taking a uniform bound for all
|f^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}(z)|
over all
i\in\mathcal{I}
(note that
\mathcal{I}
is a finite set). By Definition
2.1
, we have
\|\omega\|_{p_{t}}\leq C_{1}e^{-2t}
, where
t=d(\cdot,C_{M})
.

On the other hand,
\omega
is uniformly bounded in the convex compact subset
N_{0}:=N\setminus(\cup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\cup_{t>1}S^{i}_{t})
of
N
. Note that the function
d(\cdot,C_{M})
has a uniform upper bound on
N_{0}
. As a consequence, there is a constant
C>0
depending on
\omega
, such that

\|\omega\|_{x}\leq C\exp(-2d(x,C_{M}))~,

for all
x\in N
(compare with the first paragraph in the proof of Theorem 3.5 in
[
14
]
or the proof of Lemma 3.2, p. 106 of
[
30
]
). So
\omega
also satisfies Condition (ii).
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

For convenience, henceforth, we denote by
\omega
the representative one-forms (constructed in Lemma
3.13
) on the submanifold
N\subset M
and its universal cover
\Omega
.

3.3.
Estimates on
\bar{\omega}

The following describes the images of the infinitesimal pure translation and rotation under the map
\Psi
(see
[
40
, Lemma 3.7]
).

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
x\in(\Omega\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{3})\setminus\{O\}
and
\bar{x}:=\iota(x)\in(\bar{\Omega}\cap{\mathbb{D}}^{3})\setminus\{\bar{O}\}
. Let
R
(resp.
\bar{R}:=\Upsilon(R)
) denote the unit radial vector in
T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
) pointing away from the center. Let
v
(resp.
\bar{v}:=(\cosh\rho)\Upsilon(v)
) denote a unit lateral vector in
T_{x}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
(resp.
T_{\bar{x}}{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
), where
\rho:=d_{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}(x,O)
.

Set
r:=d_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}(\bar{x},\bar{O})
. Then

\begin{split}\Psi(R,0)&=(\bar{R},0),\\
\Psi(0,R)&=(0,\bar{R}),\\
\Psi(v,0)&=(\sqrt{1-r^{2}}\,\bar{v},-\frac{r}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\,\bar{R}\times\bar{v}),\\
\Psi(0,v)&=(0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\,\bar{v}),\end{split}

where
(R,0)
represents the infinitesimal pure translation whose value at
x
is
R
, while
(0,R)
represents the infinitesimal pure rotation whose curl at
x
is
2R
. Similarly for the other pairs of tangent vectors.

Let
\omega=d^{D}s
be the one-form on
\Omega
constructed in the proof of Lemma
3.13
and let
\bar{\omega}:=\Psi_{*}(\omega)
. We denote by
\omega^{r}_{\tau}
and
\omega^{\perp}_{\tau}
(resp.
\omega^{r}_{\sigma}
and
\omega^{\perp}_{\sigma}
) the radial and lateral components of the translation (resp. rotation) part
\omega_{\tau}
(resp.
\omega_{\sigma}
) of
\omega
, and by
\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}
and
\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\tau}}
(resp.
\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}
and
\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\sigma}}
) the radial and lateral components of the translation (resp. rotation) part
\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\tau}}
(resp.
\bar{\omega}_{\bar{\sigma}}
) of
\bar{\omega}
. The following is the estimates of their norms in the unit radial and lateral directions. The proof is similar to Lemma 3.8 in
[
40
]
. We include it here for completeness. For simplicity, we denote by
\|\cdot\|
the Euclidean norm henceforth.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
x\in(\Omega\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{3})\setminus\{O\}
and
\bar{x}:=\iota(x)\in(\bar{\Omega}\cap{\mathbb{D}}^{3})\setminus\{\bar{O}\}
. Let
R
,
\bar{R}
,
v
,
\bar{v}
,
\rho
and
r
be the same notations as in Lemma
3.16
. Then there is a constant
C>0
depending only on
\omega
, such that

\begin{split}&\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{R})\|\leq\frac{C}{1-r},\quad\quad\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{v})\|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{1-r}},\\
&\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{R})\|\leq\frac{C}{1-r},\quad\quad\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{v})\|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{1-r}},\\
&\|\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{R})\|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{1-r}},\quad\quad\|\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{v})\|\leq C,\\
&\|\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{R})\|\leq\frac{2C}{(\sqrt{1-r})^{3}},\quad\quad\|\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{v})\|\leq\frac{2C}{1-r}.\end{split}

Proof.

By the assumption and (
7
),
\bar{\omega}(d\iota(X))=(\Psi_{*}\omega)(d\iota(X))=\Psi(\omega(X))
for all
X\in T\Omega
. In particular,

\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}(d\iota(X))
is equal to the radial component of the translation part, denoted by
\big(\Psi(\omega(X))\big)^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}
, of
\Psi(\omega(X))
.
Similarly for
\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\tau}}
,
\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}
and
\bar{\omega}^{\perp}_{\bar{\sigma}}
acting on
d\iota(X)
. Note that

\bar{R}=\Upsilon(R)=\cosh^{2}\rho\cdot d\iota(R),\quad\quad\bar{v}=\cosh\rho\cdot\Upsilon(v)=\cosh\rho\cdot d\iota(v),\quad\quad r=\tanh{\rho}.

We divide the discussion into the following four cases:

Case 1
: We consider the case of
\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}
. By the above facts, we obtain

\begin{split}\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{R})\|&=\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}\big(\cosh^{2}\rho\cdot d\iota(R)\,\big)\|=\cosh^{2}{\rho}\,\|\big(\Psi({\omega}(R)\big)^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}\|\\
&=\cosh^{2}{\rho}\,\|\big(\Psi(\omega^{r}_{\tau}(R))\big)^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}\|=\cosh^{2}{\rho}\,\|\omega^{r}_{\tau}(R)\|\\
&\leq\cosh^{2}{\rho}\,\|\omega_{\tau}\|_{x}\cdot\|R\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{3}}\leq\frac{\|\omega\|_{x}}{1-r^{2}}\\
&\leq\frac{C\exp(-2d(x,C_{M}))}{1-r^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{1-r}.\end{split}

Here the first equality on the second line follows from Lemma
3.16
, which shows that the radial component of the translation part of
\Psi(\omega(R))
only comes from the radial component of the translation part of
\omega(R)
. The second equality on the second line follows from the first equality in Lemma
3.16
.
The last line is a consequence of Lemma
3.13
and the fact that
1-r^{2}\geq 1-r
. The estimate for
\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\tau}}(\bar{v})\|
follows in the same way by replacing
\bar{R}
(resp.
R
and
\cosh^{2}\rho
) by
\bar{v}
(resp.
v
and
\cosh\rho
).

Case 2
: For the estimates for
\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}
,
we have

\begin{split}\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}(\bar{R})\|&=\|\bar{\omega}^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}\big(\cosh^{2}\rho\cdot d\iota(R)\,\big)\|=\cosh^{2}{\rho}\,\|\big(\Psi({\omega}(R)\big)^{r}_{\bar{\sigma}}\|\\
