k\in\mathcal{K}
in the target domain.

It is not hard to see that
\phi_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}}
is well-defined, by the Ahlfors Finiteness Theorem
[
28
, Chap 3.1]
, Statement (4) of Proposition
2.3
and the fact that each end of a convex co-compact hyperbolic manifold admits a unique foliation by surfaces of constant (Gauss) curvature (also called
K
-foliation for simplicity)
[
26
, Theorem 2]
.
Moreover,
\phi_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}}
is smooth, see e.g.
[
8
]
.

By the Ahlfors-Bers Theorem (see
[
1
,
8
]
or
[
28
, Theorem 5.13]
),
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
has the same dimension as the space
\Pi_{l\in\mathcal{I}\sqcup\mathcal{J}\sqcup\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{l}M)
. Combined with Proposition
3.1
,
\phi_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}}
is a local diffeomorphism. Note that the principal curvatures on the surfaces homotopic to
\partial_{j}M
and
\partial_{k}M
(which have constant curvature) are uniformly bounded, so
\phi_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}}
is proper by Lemma
4.7
below. Combining the above results,
\phi_{\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}}
is a homeomorphism and the lemma follows.
∎

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

We prove here Theorem
1.6
.

We consider a compact manifold
M
with non-empty boundary, and whose interior admits a convex co-compact hyperbolic metric. Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{l}
(
1\leq l\leq n
) be an end of
M
, which is topologically the product of a closed surface
S_{l}
of genus at least
2
by
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.

Definition 4.2
.

We introduce the following notations:

•

For each
i\in\mathcal{I}
and each
c_{i}\in{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{i}M)
, let
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})\subset{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
be the subset consisting of convex co-compact metrics on
M
such that the conformal structure at infinity on
\partial_{i}M
is
c_{i}
.

•

For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
and each smooth metric
h_{j}
of curvature
K>-1
, let
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})\subset{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
be the space of convex co-compact metrics on
M
such that
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}
contains a closed surface
S_{j}
isotopic to
\partial_{j}M
with induced metric isotopic to
h_{j}
.

•

For each
k\in\mathcal{K}
and each smooth metric
h^{*}_{k}
of curvature
K<1
, with closed, contractible geodesics of length
L>2\pi
, let
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})\subset{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
be the space of convex co-compact metrics on
M
such that
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
contains a closed surface
S_{k}
isotopic to
\partial_{k}M
with third fundamental form isotopic to
h^{*}_{k}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The subsets
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
(resp.
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
) are smooth submanifolds of
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
of codimension
6\mathfrak{g}_{i}-6
(resp.
6\mathfrak{g}_{j}-6
,
6\mathfrak{g}_{k}-6
), where
\mathfrak{g}_{l}
is the genus of the boundary component
\partial_{l}M
(
1\leq l\leq n
).

Proof.

By the Ahlfors-Bers Theorem,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
is a smooth submanifold of codimension
6\mathfrak{g}_{i}-6
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Fix a constant
K\in(-1,0)
. For
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
, we consider the map

\psi^{j}_{K}:{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})\rightarrow\Pi_{l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{j\}}{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{l}M)

which sends a metric
g\in{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
on
M
to the hyperbolic metrics homothetic to the induced metrics on the
K
-surfaces isotopic to
\partial_{l}M
for
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{j\}
. Note that each end of a convex co-compact hyperbolic manifold admits a unique
K
-foliation
[
26
, Theorem 2]
,
\psi^{j}_{K}
is well-defined. By the main result in
[
40
, Theorem 0.1]
, for the prescribed metric
h_{j}
on
\partial_{j}M
, and each hyperbolic metric
h^{\prime}_{l}
on
\partial_{l}M
with
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{j\}
, there is a unique convex co-compact hyperbolic metric on
M
with the induced metric isotopic to
h_{j}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{j}M
and with the induced metric isotopic to
1/|K|h^{\prime}_{l}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{l}M
, with
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{j\}
. This implies that
\psi^{j}_{K}
is a bijection. Note that
\psi^{j}_{K}
and
(\psi^{j}_{K})^{-1}
are smooth (see e.g.
[
40
]
).
\psi^{j}_{K}
is thus a diffeomorphism. Therefore,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
is a smooth submanifold of codimension
6\mathfrak{g}_{j}-6
for each
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

For
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
, we consider the map

\psi^{k*}_{K}:{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})\rightarrow\Pi_{l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{k\}}{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{l}M)

which sends a metric
g\in{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
on
M
to the hyperbolic metrics homothetic to the third fundamental forms on the
K
-surfaces isotopic to
\partial_{l}M
for
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{k\}
. Combined with the above discussion for
\psi^{j}_{K}
and Statement (4) of Proposition
2.3
,
\psi^{k*}_{K}
is well-defined. By the main result in
[
40
, Theorem 0.2]
, for the prescribed metric
h^{*}_{k}
on
\partial_{k}M
and each hyperbolic metric
h^{\prime}_{l}
on
\partial_{l}M
with
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{k\}
, there is a unique convex co-compact hyperbolic metric on
M
with the third fundamental form isotopic to
h^{*}_{k}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{k}M
, and with the third fundamental form isotopic to
1/|K^{*}|h^{\prime}_{l}
on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{l}M
, with
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{k\}
and
K^{*}=K/(K+1)
. Note also that
\psi^{k*}_{K}
and
(\psi^{k*}_{K})^{-1}
are smooth (see e.g.
[
40
]
). Therefore,
\psi^{k*}_{K}
is a diffeomorphism and
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
is a smooth submanifold of codimension
6\mathfrak{g}_{k}-6
for each
k\in\mathcal{K}
.
∎

Definition 4.4
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{k}
(
k\geq 2
) be linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. We say that they have transversal intersection (sometimes also called normal intersection, see
[
20
, Chapter 1, §5]
) if

V_{i_{1}}+(V_{i_{2}}\cap\cdots\cap V_{i_{k}})={\mathbb{R}}^{n}~,

whenever
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}\}=\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
, or equivalently,

{\rm codim}(V_{1})+\cdots+{\rm codim}(V_{k})={\rm codim}(V_{1}\cap\cdots\cap V_{k})~.

Here the sum of the codimensions of the submanifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i}),i\in\mathcal{I}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j}),j\in\mathcal{J}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k}),k\in\mathcal{K}
is equal to the dimension of
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
. Combined with Definition
4.4
, these submanifolds intersect tranversally at a point
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
if and only if

\left(\cap_{i\in\mathcal{I}}T_{g}{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j\in\mathcal{J}}T_{g}{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})\right)\cap\left(\cap_{k\in\mathcal{K}}T_{g}{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})\right)=\{0\}~,

see
[
20
, Chapter 1, §5, p. 32]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume that
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
is an intersection point of the submanifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
over all
i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}
. Then these submanifolds intersect at
g
transversally. In particular,
g
is an isolated intersection point.

Proof.

Since the sum of the codimensions is equal to
\dim({\mathcal{CC}}(M))
, to see these submanifolds intersect transversally at
g
, it suffices to show at
g\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
,

\left(\cap_{i\in\mathcal{I}}T_{g}{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j\in\mathcal{J}}T_{g}{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})\right)\cap\left(\cap_{k\in\mathcal{K}}T_{g}{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})\right)=\{0\}~.

Suppose by contradiction that the above intersection is not zero, there is a non-trivial tangent vector
\dot{g}\in T_{g}{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
, which preserves the conformal structure
c_{i}
(on
\partial_{i}M
), the induced metric
h_{j}
(on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{j}M
), and the third fundamental form
h^{*}_{k}
(on a surface isotopic to
\partial_{k}M
) at first order for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
k\in\mathcal{K}
. This contradicts Proposition
3.1
. In particular,
g
is an isolated intersection point. The lemma follows.
∎

We will also need a compactness lemma. From here on, a “boundary data” for
M
is an
n
-tuple composed of conformal structures
c_{i}
on
\partial_{i}M
,
i\in\mathcal{I}
, metrics
h_{j}
of curvatures
K>-1
on
\partial_{j}M
,
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and metrics
h^{*}_{k}
of curvature
K<1
with closed, contractible geodesics of length
L>2\pi
on
\partial_{k}M,k\in\mathcal{K}
. We denote the set of all these boundary data (up to isotopy for each element of the
n
-tuples) by
\mathcal{X}
.

We first introduce the following estimate on the principal curvatures of convex surfaces in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of variable curvature (see e.g.
[
43
, Lemma 4.1]
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. There exists a constant
0<k_{0}<1
such that if
\Sigma\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is a convex surface with the induced metric of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
and injectivity radius bounded from below by
\epsilon
, then the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
are in
[k_{0},1/k_{0}]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
, with boundary data
X_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose that the sequence
(X_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
remain bounded in
\mathcal{X}
. Then
(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
has a convergent sub-sequence.

Proof.

Let
X_{n}=(c_{i,n},h_{j,n},h^{*}_{k,n})_{i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}}
and let
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{l}
denote the hyperbolic end of
(M,g_{n})
,
l\in\mathcal{I}\sqcup\mathcal{J}\sqcup\mathcal{K}
. Let
S^{n}_{i}
(
i\in\mathcal{I}
) denote the boundary at infinity of
(M,g_{n})
whose conformal structure is
c_{i,n}
and let
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) denote the smooth convex surface in
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{j}
(resp.
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{k}
) whose induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) is
h_{j,n}
(resp.
h^{*}_{k,n}
), with
j\in\mathcal{J}
(resp.
k\in\mathcal{K}
).

By assumption, the sequence of the induced metrics
h_{j,n}
(resp. third fundamental forms
h^{*}_{k,n}
) on
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) remains bounded in the space of
C^{\infty}
metrics on
\partial_{j}M
(resp.
\partial_{k}M
) with curvature
K>-1
(resp.
K<1
and with closed, contractible geodesics of length
L>2\pi
). Combined with Proposition
2.3
, the sequence of the induced metrics (say
h_{k,n}
) on
S^{n}_{k}
is also bounded. Note that
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) is a smooth convex closed surface of the same topological type for all
n
. Then the injectivity radius of
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) is bounded from below by some constant
\epsilon>0
for all
n
. Combining the above curvature conditions for
h_{j,n}
and
h^{*}_{k,n}
(and thus for
h_{k,n}
) and applying Lemma
4.6
to a lift of
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) to
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the principal curvatures on
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) are uniformly bounded between two positive constants.

This allows us to apply a similar analysis as Proposition 3.11 of
[
10
]
to show that the nearest point retraction from the boundary at infinity of
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{j}
(resp.
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{k}
) with the conformal hyperbolic metric to the smooth convex surface
S^{n}_{j}
(resp.
S^{n}_{k}
) with the induced metric is uniformly bi-Lipschitz for all
n
. By the relation between the first and third fundamental forms and the uniform boundedness of principal curvatures on
S^{n}_{k}
(as shown above), the duality map from
S^{n}_{k}
to its dual surface
(S^{n}_{k})^{*}
(both with respect to induced metrics) is uniformly bi-Lipschitz for all
n
. So there is a uniformly bi-Lipschitz map from the boundary at infinity of
{\mathcal{E}}^{n}_{k}
(with the conformal hyperbolic metric) to
S^{n}_{k}
(with the third fundamental form).

As a consequence, the conformal structures at infinity say
c_{l,n}
on the boundary at infinity of
{\mathcal{E}}_{l}
(
l\in\mathcal{J}\sqcup\mathcal{K}
) are also bounded. Combined with the hypothesis that the conformal structure
c_{i,n}
on
S^{n}_{i}
are bounded, it follows from the Ahlfors-Bers theorem
[
8
]
that the sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of convex co-compact metrics realizing the boundary data
X_{n}
is also bounded, and thus has a convergent sub-sequence.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Let
X^{0}=(c_{i,0},h_{j,0},h^{*}_{k,0})_{i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}}
be a boundary data for
M
. We assume that
X^{0}
is realized by a certain metric
g_{0}\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
. For simplicity, we denote
c_{i,0}=c_{i}
,
h_{j,0}=h_{j}
and
h^{*}_{k,0}=h^{*}_{k}
.

Similarly, let
X^{1}=(c_{i,1},h_{j,1},h^{*}_{k,1})_{i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}}
be another boundary data of the same type. We then choose a smooth 1-parameter family
(X^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\mathcal{X}
connecting
X^{0}
to
X^{1}
. We denote by
U\subset[0,1]
the maximal subset of
[0,1]
containing
0
such that
X^{t}
is realized as the boundary data of a metric
g_{t}\in{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
for all
t\in U
.

Note that under the smooth variation of the boundary data
c_{i}
,
h_{j}
and
h^{*}_{k}
determined by
X^{t}
, the submanifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
vary smoothly in
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
(this can be seen by the smoothness of the parametrization map in the proof of Ahlfors-Bers Theorem
[
8
]
and the main results in
[
40
]
). By Lemma
4.5
, the submanifolds
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
over all
i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}
intersect transversally at
g_{0}
(with
g_{0}
isolated). So the submanifolds
{\mathcal{U}}^{t}_{i}(c_{i,t})
,
{\mathcal{V}}^{t}_{j}(h_{j,t})
,
{\mathcal{W}}^{t}_{k}(h^{*}_{k,t})
corresponding to the smooth variation
c_{i,t},h_{j,t},h^{*}_{k,t}
determined by
X^{t}
keep intersecting each other for
t
sufficiently small, with each intersection point isolated and varying smoothly in
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
. Therefore,
U
is open. By Lemma
4.7
,
U
is also closed, so
U=[0,1]
.

It follows from this argument that any metric realizing
X^{0}
can be deformed in
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
uniquely (once the path
(X^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is chosen) to a metric realizing
X^{1}
. Note that
{\mathcal{CC}}(M)
is path-connected. So each boundary data can be realized, and moreover the number of realizations is the same for all boundary data, since each intersection point (which realizes the boundary data
X^{0}
) of
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}(c_{i})
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(h_{j})
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}(h^{*}_{k})
over all
i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J},k\in\mathcal{K}
varies smoothly and keeps isolated under the smooth variation
(X^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
.

However we already know from Lemma
4.1
that for constant curvature metrics this number is
1
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

5.
Convex domains in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}

We turn in this section to convex domains in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. We will be using the results obtained in the previous section and obtain results on convex domains in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
by approximating them, in a suitable sense, by convex domains in quasifuchsian manifolds.

5.1.
The classical conformal welding and generalized gluing maps

5.1.1.
The classical conformal welding of a Jordan curve in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}

Let
C\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
be an oriented Jordan curve. We denote by
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
the connected components of
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus C
on the left and right sides of
C
, which corresponds to the positive and negative sides (or the “upper” and “lower” sides) of
C
. By the Riemann mapping theorem, there is a conformal (or bi-holomorphic) map, say
\varphi^{+}_{C}
(resp.
\varphi^{-}_{C}
), that maps the upper (resp. lower) half-plane of
{\mathbb{C}}
to
U^{+}
(resp.
U^{-}
). Let
\partial\varphi^{+}_{C}
(resp.
\partial\varphi^{-}_{C}
) denote the boundary correspondence (this is a homeomorphism by Carathéodory’s theorem). The classical
conformal welding
is defined as
(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{+}_{C}
, which is well-defined up to pre- and post-composition by elements of
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. For simplicity, we consider the
normalized
representative (that is, it preserves
0,1,\infty\in\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\cong{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
) and denote it by
\Phi_{C}
(see e.g.
[
10
]
).

We first recall the notion of quasi-circles and quasisymmetric maps.

Definition 5.1
.

A Jordan curve
C\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
is said to be a
k
-quasi-circle (
k\geq 1
) if it is the image of
{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
under a
k
-quasiconformal homeomorphism from
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
to itself. We say
C
is normalized if it goes through the three points
0,1,\infty
.

Definition 5.2
.

An orientation-preserving homeomorphism
f:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is called
k
-quasisymmetric (
k\geq 1
) if it is the boundary extension of a
k
-quasiconformal homeomorphism from
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
to itself.

We say a quasisymmetric homeomorphism
f
is quasifuchsian if there is a surface group
\pi_{1}(S)
and two Fuchsian representations
\rho_{i}:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
(
i=1,2
) such that
f
is
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
-equivariant. More precisely,

f(\rho_{1}(\gamma)x)=\rho_{2}(\gamma)f(x),

for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
and
x\in{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
.

Let
{\mathcal{QC}}
denote the space of normalized quasi-circles in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
(with the Hausdorff topology) and let
{\mathcal{T}}
denote the universal Teichmüller space, which is the space of normalized quasisymmetric homeomorphisms of
{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
. The following is a classical result of Ahlfors and Bers
[
3
,
2
]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
C\in{\mathcal{QC}}
be a normalized
k
-quasi-circle. Then the conformal welding
\Phi_{C}
of
C
is a normalized
k
-quasisymmetric homeomorphism in
{\mathcal{T}}
. Conversely, given a normalized
k
-quasisymmetric homeomorphism
f\in{\mathcal{T}}
, there is a unique normalized
k
-quasi-circle in
{\mathcal{QC}}
whose conformal welding is
f
.

Recall that the concepts of complex structure and conformal structure are equivalent on two-dimensional oriented Riemannian manifolds (see e.g.
[
24
, Theorem 1.7]
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
be the Riemann surfaces induced by oriented Riemannian surfaces
(S_{1},h_{1})
and
(S_{2},h_{2})
, respectively. Then
f:(S_{1},h_{1})\rightarrow(S_{2},h_{2})
is an orientation-preserving conformal map if and only if
f:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
is a bi-holomorphic map.

5.1.2.
The gluing maps for unbounded convex domains in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of the first type

We now consider the first type of unbounded convex domain (see Section
1.2
). Let
\Omega
be a convex subset in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
with its convex boundary
\partial\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
an open complete disk and its ideal boundary
\partial_{\infty}\Omega\subset(\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\cong{\mathbb{C}P}^{1})
a closed disk. We denote by
\partial(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)
the boundary of
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
in
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. Then
\partial(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)=\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
is a Jordan curve in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
.

Let
I
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
) denote the induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) on
\partial\Omega
and let
{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}
(resp.
{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}
) denote the upper (resp. lower) half-plane in
{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\Omega
be a bi-holomorphic map. We denote by
h^{\pm}_{0}
the hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}
. Let
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega
be a map such that
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{*}I
is conformal to
h^{-}_{0}
and let
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega
be a map such that
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})^{*}I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
is conformal to
h^{-}_{0}
. By the Carathéodory’s theorem and Lemma
5.4
, each of the maps
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}
,
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}
and
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
extends to a homeomorphism on the boundary of
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
. We define the “conformal welding” between the conformal structure on
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
and the conformal structure on
\partial\Omega
induced by
I
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
), called the
I
-gluing map
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map
) of
\Omega
in the following.

Definition 5.5
.

The
I
-gluing map of
\Omega
is defined as

\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}:=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}.

The
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map of
\Omega
is defined as

\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}:=(\partial\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}~.

Here the maps
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}
,
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}
and
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
are always chosen such that the above gluing maps are
normalized
, that is, preserving
0,1,\infty
.

We stress that under the normalization condition, the above pair
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})
(resp.
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})
) is unique, up to left composition with a common element of
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
. In particular, the
I
- (resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-) gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
(resp.
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
) of
\Omega
is independent of the choice of the representative pair
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})
(resp.
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})
). The above definition is well-defined.

Example 5.6
.

If
\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega)=\partial(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)
is a round circle in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
, and the induced metric (or third fundamental form) on
\partial\Omega
has constant curvature, then the gluing maps
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
and
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
are both the identity.

If
\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega)=\partial(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)
is a Jordan curve invariant under the group
\Gamma_{\rho}
given by the quasifuchsian representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, then
\Omega/\Gamma_{\rho}
is a convex subset in the quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}/\Gamma_{\rho}
with one conformal boundary surface
(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}
and one convex boundary surface
(\partial\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}
.

The map
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega})^{-1}:\partial_{\infty}\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}
conjugates the action of
\rho
on
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
to a properly discontinuous action by isometries on
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
, which is the Fuchsian representation
\rho_{c}
of the complex structure
c
on
(\partial_{\infty}\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}:

(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\rho\circ\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}=\rho_{c}.

The map
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}:\partial\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}
(resp.
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})^{-1}:\partial\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}
) conjugates the action of
\rho
on
\partial\Omega
to a properly discontinuous action by isometries on
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
, which is the Fuchsian representation
\rho_{I}
(resp.
\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}
) of the hyperbolic metric in the conformal class of the induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) on
(\partial\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}
(via the Riemannian uniformization theorem):

(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\rho\circ\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}=\rho_{I}\quad\quad(resp.\,\,(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\rho\circ\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}=\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}\,\,).

Combined with Definition
5.5
, we obtain that

\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}\circ\rho_{c}=\rho_{I}\circ\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}\quad\quad(resp.\,\,\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}\circ\rho_{c}=\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}\circ\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}\,\,).

Therefore,
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
(resp.
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
) is a
(\rho_{c},\rho_{I})
-equivariant (resp.
(\rho_{c},\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI})
-equivariant) quasisymmetric homeomorphism.

5.1.3.
The gluing maps for unbounded convex domains in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of the second type

For the second type of unbounded convex domain (see Section
1.2
), let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
be a convex domain such that
\partial\Omega
is the disjoint union of two complete disks, denoted here by
\partial_{-}\Omega
and
\partial_{+}\Omega
, which “meet” at infinity along a Jordan curve, oriented such that
\partial_{+}\Omega
and
\partial_{-}\Omega
lie on its left and right-hand sides respectively (see e.g.
[
10
]
).

In this case,
\partial_{\infty}(\partial_{-}\Omega)=\partial_{\infty}(\partial_{+}\Omega)=\partial_{\infty}\Omega
is a Jordan curve. Let
I_{\pm}
and
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}
denote the induced metric and third fundamental form on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
. Let
\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow\partial_{\pm}\Omega
be a map such that
(\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{*}(I_{\pm})
is conformal to
h^{\pm}_{0}
and let
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow\partial_{\pm}\Omega
be a map such that
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{*}(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm})
is conformal to
h^{\pm}_{0}
. Note that each of the maps
\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega}
and
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}}_{\Omega}
extends to a homeomorphism on the boundary of
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
, by Lemma
5.4
and Caratheodory’s theorem.

In the following, we define the “conformal welding” between the conformal structure on
\partial_{+}\Omega
induced by
I_{+}
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}
) and the conformal structure on
\partial_{-}\Omega
induced by
I_{-}
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{-}
), called the
I
-gluing map
(resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map
) of
\Omega
. Besides, we can also define a “conformal welding” between the conformal structure on
\partial_{+}\Omega
induced by
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}
and the conformal structure on
\partial_{-}\Omega
induced by
I_{-}
, called the
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-mixed gluing map
of
\Omega
(see more precisely below).

Definition 5.7
.

The
I
-gluing map of
\Omega
is defined as

\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}:=(\partial\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}.

The
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map of
\Omega
is defined as

\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}:=(\partial\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{-}}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}.

The
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-mixed gluing map of
\Omega
is defined as

\Phi^{(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)}_{\Omega}:=(\partial\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}~.

Here the maps
\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega}
and
\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}}_{\Omega}
are also chosen such that the above gluing maps are
normalized
.

We stress that under the normalization condition, the above pair
(\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})
(resp.
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{-}}_{\Omega})
,
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}},\varphi^{I_{-}})
) is unique, up to left composition with a common element of
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
. As a consequence, the
I
- (resp.
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-,
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-) gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
(resp.
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
,
\Phi^{(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)}_{\Omega}
) of
\Omega
is independent of the choice of the representative pair
(\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})
(resp.
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}}_{\Omega},\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{-}}_{\Omega})
,
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{+}},\varphi^{I_{-}})
). The above definition is well-defined. Similarly we can also define the
(I,I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI)
-mixed gluing map of
\Omega
. Without loss of generality, we only consider the
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-mixed gluing map here.

Example 5.8
.

If
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
is a round circle in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
, and the induced metric (or third fundamental form) on
\partial\Omega
has constant curvature, the gluing maps
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
,
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
and
\Phi^{(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)}_{\Omega}
are the identity. If
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
is a Jordan curve invariant under the group
\Gamma_{\rho}
given by a quasifuchsian representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, then
\Omega/\Gamma_{\rho}
is a convex subset in the quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}/\Gamma_{\rho}
with two convex boundary surfaces
(\partial_{\pm}\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}
.

Similarly as Example
5.8
, the map
(\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{-1}:\partial_{\pm}\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}
(resp.
(\varphi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{-1}:\partial_{\pm}\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}
) conjugates the action of
\rho
on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
to the Fuchsian representation
\rho_{I^{\pm}}
(resp.
\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI^{\pm}}
) of the hyperbolic metric in the conformal class of the induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) on
(\partial_{\pm}\Omega)/\Gamma_{\rho}
. As a consequence, the gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
(resp.
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
) is a
(\rho_{I^{+}},\rho_{I^{-}})
-equivariant (resp.
(\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI^{+}},\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI^{-}})
-equivariant) quasisymmetric homeomorphism. The mixed gluing map
\Phi^{(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)}_{\Omega}
is a
(\rho_{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI^{+}},\rho_{I^{-}})
-equivariant quasisymmetric homeomorphism.

The above gluing maps can be viewed as generalized versions of the classical conformal welding.

5.1.4.
Approximation of quasisymmetric maps

It is shown in
[
11
, Lemma 3.4]
and
[
10
, Proposition 9.1]
that a quasisymmetric homeomorphism can be approximated by a sequence of quasifuchsian quasisymmetric homeomorphisms:

Proposition 5.9
.

Any quasisymmetric homeomorphism
f:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is the
C^{0}
-limit of a sequence of uniformly quasisymmetric quasifuchsian homeomorphisms
f_{n}
.

5.2.
Fuchsian approximations of non-positively curved metrics

For the convenience of construction, in this subsection we use the Poincaré unit disk model of
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
, which is the unit open disk
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|<1\}
with the Poincaré metric

h_{0}=\frac{4}{(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}|dz|^{2}.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
h
be a smooth, complete conformal metric on an open disk
D\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
and let
\rho:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
be a Fuchsian representation. We say that
h
is
\rho
-invariant if there is a conformal map
\varphi:{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\rightarrow D
with
\varphi^{*}h=e^{2u}h_{0}
, where
u:{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function, such that

e^{2u(\rho(\gamma)x)}h_{0}(\rho(\gamma)x)=e^{2u(x)}h_{0}(x),

for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
and
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
h^{*}
be a smooth, complete, bounded
conformal metric of curvature
K_{h^{*}}\in[-1/\epsilon,0]
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
and let
(\rho^{*}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of Fuchsian representations of
\Gamma_{n}:=\pi_{1}(S_{n})
whose fundamental domain contains a closed hyperbolic disk
B_{H}(0,\tau_{n})
centered at
0
with radius
\tau_{n}
, where
\tau_{n}
strictly increases to
\infty
. Then there is a sequence of
\rho^{*}_{n}
-invariant conformal metrics, say
h^{*}_{n}
, on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, with curvature
K_{h^{*}_{n}}\in[-1/\epsilon^{\prime},0]
(for some
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
), which uniformly converges to
h^{*}
smoothly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
P^{*}_{n}
denote the fundamental domain of
\rho^{*}_{n}
that contains the closed hyperbolic disk
B_{H}(0,\tau_{n})
. Let
u^{*}:{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth function such that
h^{*}=e^{2u^{*}}h_{0}
. Since
h^{*}
is a complete, bounded (see Definition
1.1
) conformal metric with curvature at least
-1/\epsilon
, combined with
[
29
, Theorem 2.8]
, there exist constants
c^{*}_{1}>0>c^{*}_{0}=\log\sqrt{\epsilon}
such that
c^{*}_{0}\leq u^{*}\leq c^{*}_{1}
.
If
u^{*}
is constant on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, we set
v^{*}_{n}:=u^{*}
. If
u^{*}
is not constant on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, without loss of generality, we assume that
u^{*}
is not constant on
B_{H}(0,\tau_{1})
. We construct the desired
h^{*}_{n}
using the following steps:

Step 1
: For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we construct a smooth
v^{*}_{n}:{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
which satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

c^{*}_{0}\leq v^{*}_{n}<c^{*}_{1}
on
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
, where
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
denote the open euclidean disk of radius
t_{n}
and
t_{n}=\tanh(\tau_{n}/2)
.

(ii)

v^{*}_{n}
is equal to
c^{*}_{1}
outside
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
, and equal to
c^{*}_{0}
in
B(0,l_{n})
, for a sequence
(l_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
(to be determined) such that
l_{n}<t_{n}
and
l_{n}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.

(iii)

v^{*}_{n}
is rotationally symmetric (i.e.
v^{*}_{n}
is invariant under the rotation centered at
0
) on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

(iv)

There is a constant
C>1
such that on
\mathring{B}_{H}(0,t_{n})\setminus B_{H}(0,l_{n})
, the (Gaussian) curvature of
e^{2v^{*}_{n}}h_{0}
satisfies

-C/\epsilon\leq e^{-2v^{*}_{n}}(\Delta_{h_{0}}v^{*}_{n}-1)\leq 0~,

for
n
sufficiently large, where
\Delta_{h_{0}}v^{*}_{n}:=-tr(D^{h_{0}}dv^{*}_{n})=-((1-|z|^{2})^{2}/4)(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})v^{*}_{n}
, and
D^{h_{0}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h_{0}
.

We will construct
v_{n}^{*}
as a function on
|z|:=r\in[0,1)
– which is then extended to a rotationally invariant function on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
– depending on the parameter
t_{n}\in(0,1)
and will show that, if
n
is sufficiently large, then
v_{n}^{*}
satisfies conditions (i)–(iv). Note that
v_{n}^{*}
satisfies condition (i) and thanks to the expression of the Laplace operator for radial functions, to show condition (iv), it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C>1
such that

(36)

\frac{C}{\epsilon}e^{2c_{0}^{*}}\geq\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}((v_{n}^{*})^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}(v_{n}^{*})^{\prime}(r))\geq-1~,

for
n
sufficiently large.

Given
s\in(0,1)
, we define
l,t\in[0,1]
such that
l<s<t
and that

(37)

\frac{2}{1-s}=\frac{1}{1-l}+\frac{1}{1-t}~,

(38)

\frac{(1-l)(1-t)}{(1-s)^{2}}=\exp(2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0}))~.

Note that there is indeed a unique possible of
l,t
satisfying those conditions.
This is more easily seen by setting
l^{\prime}=1/(1-l),s^{\prime}=1/(1-s),t^{\prime}=1/(1-t)
, since the conditions then become

2s^{\prime}=l^{\prime}+t^{\prime}~,~~l^{\prime}t^{\prime}=s^{\prime 2}\exp(-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0}))~,

and if we further set

d=s^{\prime}-l^{\prime}=t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}

then
l^{\prime}t^{\prime}=s^{\prime 2}-d^{2}
and the second equation can be written as

s^{\prime 2}-d^{2}=s^{\prime 2}\exp(-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0}))~,

which uniquely determines
d
, and therefore
l^{\prime},t^{\prime}
and
l,t
. More precisely,

d=s^{\prime}\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}},\quad l^{\prime}=s^{\prime}(1-\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}}),\quad t^{\prime}=s^{\prime}(1+\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}})~.

Moreover it is important to note that
l,t\to 1
as
s\to 1
, since as
s\to 1
,
s^{\prime}\to\infty
,
d\to\infty
proportionally to
s^{\prime}
, and therefore
l^{\prime},t^{\prime}\to\infty
proportionally to
s^{\prime}
, too.

We now define
v_{n}^{*}
as follows. For each
t_{n}=\tanh(\tau_{n}/2)
, set

s_{n}:=1-(1-t_{n})(1+\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}})~,

l_{n}:=1-(1-s_{n})(1-\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}})^{-1}=1-e^{2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}(1-t_{n})(1+\sqrt{1-e^{-2(c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0})}})^{2}~.

Then
l_{n},s_{n},t_{n}
satisfies
0<l_{n}<s_{n}<t_{n}<1
, (
37
) and (
38
).

•

For
r\in[0,l_{n}]
,
v_{n}^{*}(r)=c_{0}^{*}
,

•

for
r\in[l_{n},s_{n})
,
v_{n}^{*}(r)=f_{n}(r)=(1/2)(\log((1-l_{n})/(1-r))-(r-l_{n})/(1-l_{n}))+c^{*}_{0}
,

•

for
r\in[s_{n},t_{n})
,
v_{n}^{*}(r)=g_{n}(r)=(1/2)(\log((1-r)/(1-t_{n}))+(r-t_{n})/(1-t_{n}))+c^{*}_{1}
,

•

for
r\in[t_{n},1)
,
v_{n}^{*}(r)=c^{*}_{1}
.

It is easy to check that
f_{n}(l_{n})=c^{*}_{0}
and that
g_{n}(t_{n})=c^{*}_{1}
. Moreover,

\begin{split}g_{n}(s_{n})-f_{n}(s_{n})&=\frac{1}{2}(\log\frac{1-s_{n}}{1-t_{n}}+\frac{s_{n}-t_{n}}{1-t_{n}})+c^{*}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}(\log\frac{1-l_{n}}{1-s_{n}}-\frac{s_{n}-l_{n}}{1-l_{n}})-c^{*}_{0}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\frac{(1-s_{n})^{2}}{(1-t_{n})(1-l_{n})}+1-\frac{1-s_{n}}{1-t_{n}}+1-\frac{1-s_{n}}{1-l_{n}}\right)+c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{(1-s_{n})^{2}}{(1-t_{n})(1-l_{n})}+c^{*}_{1}-c^{*}_{0}~,\end{split}

where the last step used (
37
). However (
38
) then shows that the right-hand side is zero, so that
f_{n}(s_{n})=g_{n}(s_{n})
.

One can also check quite directly that

f^{\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-r}-\frac{1}{1-l_{n}}\right)~,

g^{\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{1-r}+\frac{1}{1-t_{n}}\right)~,

so that
f^{\prime}_{n}(l_{n})=g^{\prime}_{n}(t_{n})=0
.
Moreover,
f^{\prime}_{n}(s_{n})=g^{\prime}_{n}(s_{n})
by (
37
).

The second derivatives of
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
can also be computed quite directly.

f^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2(1-r)^{2}}~,~~g^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)=-\frac{1}{2(1-r)^{2}}~.

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}_{n}(r)}{r}+f^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{8}\left(1+\frac{1-r}{r}(1-\frac{1-r}{1-l_{n}})\right)~,

for
r\in[l_{n},s_{n}]
. Note that on
[l_{n},s_{n}]
the right-hand side is greater than
0
and less than
(1-l_{n})/l_{n}+1
(which is uniformly bounded for sufficiently large
n
, since
l_{n}\rightarrow 1
). So the restriction of
v^{*}_{n}
to
[l_{n},s_{n}]
satisfies (
36
).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{g^{\prime}_{n}(r)}{r}+g^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{8}\left(-1+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{r}(\frac{1}{1-t_{n}}-\frac{1}{1-r})\right)~,

for
r\in[s_{n},t_{n}]
. Note that on
[s_{n},t_{n}]
the right-hand side is greater than
-1/2
and less than the following:

\frac{1-s_{n}}{1-t_{n}}\cdot\frac{t_{n}-s_{n}}{s_{n}}~,

which is uniformly bounded for sufficiently large
n
, since by definition
1-t_{n}
is proportional to
1-s_{n}
, and also
0<s_{n}<t_{n}<1
with
s_{n}\rightarrow 1
. So the restriction of
v^{*}_{n}
to
[s_{n},t_{n}]
satisfies (
36
). Moreover, the restriction of
v^{*}_{n}
to
[0,l_{n}]
and
[t_{n},1)
clearly satisfies (
36
).

With an additional smoothing by convolution,
v^{*}_{n}
can be replaced by a smooth function while changing its first and second derivatives by at most a small constant. Since the convolution of two functions has the derivative property that
(f\ast g)^{(n)}=f^{(n)}\ast g
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, one can choose an appropriate convolution such that the new obtained function (still denote by
v^{*}_{n}
) satisfies (i)-(iv). This concludes the proof of Step 1.

Step 2
: Let
w^{*}_{n}:=\max\{u^{*},v^{*}_{n}\}
. Then
w_{n}
is equal to
u^{*}
on
B(0,l_{n})
, and equal to
c^{*}_{1}
outside
B(0,t_{n})
. So the metric
e^{2w^{*}_{n}}h_{0}
has constant curvature
-1/e^{2c^{*}_{1}}
outside
B(0,t_{n})
. At each point
z\in B(0,t_{n})
where
u^{*}
and
v^{*}_{n}
take the same value,
w^{*}_{n}(z)<c^{*}_{1}
and might not be differentiable there. However since
w^{*}_{n}
is defined as the maximum of two functions, the restriction of
w^{*}_{n}
to any curve transverse to the locus where
u^{*}=v^{*}_{n}
has a non-negative jump in its second derivative there. This implies that
(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})w^{*}_{n}
is bounded from below by a non-negative constant (in a distributional sense). We can therefore smooth
w^{*}_{n}
(e.g. by convolution) in a neighborhood
U_{z}
of the locus where
u^{*}=v^{*}_{n}
, to obtain a new function (denoted by
\hat{w}^{*}_{n}
) such that
\hat{w}^{*}_{n}
is smooth, strictly convex and
satisfies the following in
U_{z}
:

\frac{C}{\epsilon}e^{2c_{0}^{*}}\geq\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})\hat{w}_{n}^{*}\geq-1~,

for a constant
C>1
.

Therefore, we obtain a smooth metric
e^{2\hat{w}^{*}_{n}}h_{0}
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
with curvature
-C/\epsilon\leq K_{e^{2\hat{w}^{*}_{n}}h_{0}}\leq 0
, which coincides with
h^{*}
on
\mathring{B}(0,l_{n})
and has constant curvature
-1/e^{2c^{*}_{1}}
outside
B(0,t_{n})
.

Step 3
: Now we construct
h^{*}_{n}
in the following way: for any
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{n}
, we push forward the metric
e^{2\hat{w}^{*}_{n}}h_{0}
on
P_{n}
to
\rho^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n}
by
\rho^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{n})
. Note that the metric on each
\rho^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n}
extends to the boundary
\partial(\rho^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n})
in the
C^{\infty}
-sense, since they all have constant curvature
-1/e^{2c^{*}_{1}}
at the points sufficiently close to
\partial(\rho^{*}_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n})
.

By construction,
h^{*}_{n}
is
\rho^{*}_{n}
-invariant. Moreover, for each compact subset
K^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, there is
N^{\prime}=N^{\prime}(K^{\prime})>0
sufficiently large, such that
K^{\prime}\subset\mathring{B}(0,l_{N^{\prime}})
, and thus
h^{*}_{n}=h^{*}
on
K^{\prime}
for all
n\geq N^{\prime}
. Therefore,
h^{*}_{n}
uniformly converges to
h^{*}
smoothly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.
∎

5.3.
Fuchsian approximations of metrics with
K>-1

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
h
be a smooth, complete, bounded
conformal metric of curvature
K_{h}\in[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
and let
(\rho_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of Fuchsian representations of surface groups
\Gamma_{n}:=\pi_{1}(S_{n})
whose fundamental domain contains a closed hyperbolic disk
B_{H}(0,\tau_{n})
centered at
0
with radius
\tau_{n}
, where
\tau_{n}
strictly increases to
\infty
. Then there is a sequence of
\rho_{n}
-invariant conformal metrics, say
h_{n}
, on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, with curvature
K_{h_{n}}\in[-1+\epsilon^{\prime},1/\epsilon^{\prime}]
for some
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, which uniformly converges to
h
smoothly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
P_{n}
denote the fundamental domain of
\rho_{n}
that contains the closed hyperbolic disk
B_{H}(0,\tau_{n})
. Let
u:{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth function such that
h=e^{2u}h_{0}
. Note that
h
is a complete, bounded (see Definition
1.1
) conformal metric with curvature at least
-1+\epsilon
. Then there exist constants
c_{1}>c_{0}:=-\log\sqrt{1-\epsilon}>0
such that
c_{0}\leq u\leq c_{1}
(see
[
29
, Theorem 2.8]
).
If
u
is constant on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, we set
v_{n}:=u
. If
u
is not constant on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, without loss of generality, we assume that
u
is not constant on
B_{H}(0,\tau_{1})
. We construct the desired
h_{n}
using the following steps:

Step 1
:
For each
n
, we construct a smooth function
v_{n}:{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

c_{0}<v_{n}\leq c_{1}
on
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
, where
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
denote the open euclidean disk of radius
t_{n}
and
t_{n}=\tanh(\tau_{n}/2)
.

(ii)

v_{n}
is equal to
c_{0}
outside
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})
, and equal to
c_{1}
in
B(0,l_{n})
, for a sequence
(l_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
(to be determined) such that
l_{n}<t_{n}
and
l_{n}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.

(iii)

v_{n}
is rotationally symmetric (i.e.
v_{n}
is invariant under the rotation centered at
0
).

(iv)

On
\mathring{B}(0,t_{n})\setminus B_{H}(0,l_{n})
, there is a constant
C>1
such that the (Gaussian) curvature of
e^{2v_{n}}h_{0}
satisfies

-1+(\epsilon/C)\leq K_{e^{2v_{n}}h_{0}}=e^{-2v_{n}}(\Delta_{h_{0}}v_{n}-1)\leq C/\epsilon,

for
n
sufficiently large.

Similarly as the construction in Proposition
5.11
, we will construct
v_{n}
as a function on
|z|:=r\in[0,1)
– which is then extended to a rotationally invariant function on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
satisfying conditions (i)–(iv). Note that
v_{n}
satisfies condition (i) with
v_{n}\geq c_{0}>0
and thanks to the expression of the Laplace operator for radial functions, to show condition (iv), it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C>1
such that

(39)

e^{2c_{0}}(1-\epsilon/C)\geq\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}(v_{n}^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}v^{\prime}_{n}(r))\geq-(C/\epsilon)e^{2c_{0}}-1~,

for
n
sufficiently large.

Replacing
c^{*}_{0},c^{*}_{1}
by
c_{0},c_{1}
in (
38
) and using the corresponding
l_{n},s_{n},t_{n}
as defined in the proof of Proposition
5.11
(with
c^{*}_{0},c^{*}_{1}
replaced by
c_{0},c_{1}
), we construct
v_{n}
below:

•

For
r\in[0,l_{n}]
,
v_{n}(r)=c_{1}
,

•

for
r\in[l_{n},s_{n})
,
v_{n}(r)=f_{n}(r)=(1/2)(\log((1-r)/(1-l_{n}))+(r-l_{n})/(1-l_{n}))+c_{1}
,

•

for
r\in[s_{n},t_{n})
,
v_{n}(r)=g_{n}(r)=(1/2)(\log((1-t_{n})/(1-r))-(r-t_{n})/(1-t_{n}))+c_{0}
,

•

for
r\in[t_{n},1)
,
v_{n}(r)=c_{0}
.

One can check by a direct computation that
f_{n}(l_{n})=c_{1}
,
g_{n}(t_{n})=c_{0}
and
f_{n}(s_{n})=g_{n}(s_{n})
. Furthermore,

f^{\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{1-r}+\frac{1}{1-l_{n}}\right)~,\quad g^{\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{1-r}-\frac{1}{1-t_{n}}\right)~.

Then
f^{\prime}_{n}(l_{n})=g^{\prime}_{n}(t_{n})=0
and
f^{\prime}_{n}(s_{n})=g^{\prime}_{n}(s_{n})
. Moreover,

f^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)=-\frac{1}{2(1-r)^{2}}~,~~g^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)=\frac{1}{2(1-r)^{2}}~.

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}_{n}(r)}{r}+f^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{8}\left(-1+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{r}\left(\frac{1}{1-l_{n}}-\frac{1}{1-r}\right)\right)~,

for
r\in[l_{n},s_{n}]
. Note that on
[l_{n},s_{n}]
the right-hand side is less than
0
and greater than the following:

-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1-l_{n}}{1-s_{n}}\frac{s_{n}-l_{n}}{2l_{n}}~,

which is uniformly bounded for sufficiently large
n
, using the facts that
1-l_{n}
is proportional to
1-s_{n}
(by definition),
0<l_{n}<s_{n}<1
and
l_{n}\rightarrow 1
. So the restriction of
v_{n}
to
[l_{n},s_{n}]
satisfies (
39
).
Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{g^{\prime}_{n}(r)}{r}+g^{\prime\prime}_{n}(r)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{8}\left(1+\frac{1-r}{r}\left(1-\frac{1-r}{1-t_{n}}\right)\right)~,

for
r\in[s_{n},t_{n}]
. Note that on
[s_{n},t_{n}]
the right-hand side is less than
1/2
and greater than the following:

\frac{1}{8}-\frac{1-s_{n}}{1-t_{n}}\cdot\frac{t_{n}-s_{n}}{2s_{n}}~,

which is uniformly bounded for sufficiently large
n
, using the facts that
1-t_{n}
is proportional to
1-s_{n}
(by definition),
0<s_{n}<t_{n}<1
and
s_{n}\rightarrow 1
. So the restriction of
v_{n}
to
[s_{n},t_{n}]
satisfies (
39
). Moreover, the restriction of
v_{n}
to
[0,l_{n}]
and
[t_{n},1)
clearly satisfies (
39
).

As discussed in Proposition
5.11
, one can apply to
v_{n}
an appropriate convolution such that the new function (still denote by
v_{n}
) satisfies (i)-(iv). This concludes the proof of Step 1.

Step 2
: Let
w_{n}:=\min\{u,v_{n}\}
. Then
w_{n}
is equal to
u
on
B(0,l_{n})
, and equal to
c_{0}
outside
B(0,t_{n})
. So the metric
e^{2w_{n}}h_{0}
has constant curvature
-1+\epsilon
outside
B(0,t_{n})
. At each point
z\in B(0,t_{n})
where
u
and
v_{n}
take the same value,
w_{n}(z)>c_{0}
but might not be differentiable there. However since
w_{n}
is defined as the minimum of two functions, the restriction of
w_{n}
to any curve transverse to the locus where
u=v_{n}
has a non-positive jump in its second derivative there. This implies that
(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})w_{n}
is bounded from above by a non-positive constant (in a distributional sense). We can therefore smooth
w_{n}
(e.g. by convolution) in the neighborhood of the locus where
u=v_{n}
, to obtain a new obtained function denoted by
\hat{w}_{n}
) such that
\hat{w}_{n}
is smooth, strictly concave and satisfies the following in
U_{z}
:

e^{2c_{0}}(1-\epsilon/C)\geq\frac{(1-r^{2})^{2}}{4}(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy})\hat{w}_{n}\geq-(C/\epsilon)e^{2c_{0}}-1~,

for a constant
C>1
.

Therefore, we obtain a smooth metric
e^{2\hat{w}_{n}}h_{0}
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
with curvature
-1+(\epsilon/C)\leq K_{e^{2\hat{w}_{n}}h_{0}}\leq C/\epsilon
, which coincides with
h
on
\mathring{B}(0,l_{n})
and has constant curvature
-1+\epsilon
outside
B(0,t_{n})
.

Step 3
: Now we construct
h_{n}
in the following way: for any
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{n}
, we push forward the metric
e^{2\hat{w}_{n}}h_{0}
on
P_{n}
to
\rho_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n}
by
\rho_{n}(\gamma_{n})
. Note that the metric on each
\rho_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n}
extends to the boundary
\partial(\rho_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n})
in the
C^{\infty}
-sense, since they all have constant curvature
-1+\epsilon
at the points sufficiently close to
\partial(\rho_{n}(\gamma_{n})P_{n})
.

By construction,
h_{n}
is
\rho_{n}
-invariant. Moreover, for each compact subset
K\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, there is
N=N(K)>0
sufficiently large, such that
K\subset\mathring{B}(0,l_{N})
, and thus
h_{n}=h
on
K
for all
n\geq N
. Therefore,
h_{n}
uniformly converges to
h
smoothly on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.
∎

5.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Let
D_{-},D_{+}\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
be disjoint quasi-disks with
\partial D_{-}=\partial D_{+}
, denoted by
C
for simplicity, which is oriented such that
D_{+}
and
D_{-}
lie on the left and right sides of
C
. By definition,
C
is a quasi-circle. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and let
h_{-}
(resp.
h_{+}
) be a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metric of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
D_{-}
(resp.
D_{+}
), and
h^{*}_{-}
(resp.
h^{*}_{+}
) be a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metric of curvature varying in
[-1/\epsilon,0]
on
D_{-}
(resp.
D_{+}
).

5.4.1.
Equivalent statements of Theorem
1.2

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\Phi_{C}
be the classical conformal welding of
C
. The statements (i)-(v) in Theorem
1.2
are respectively equivalent to the following:

(I)

There is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of first type with the induced metric on
\partial\Omega
isometric to
h_{-}
, and the
I
-gluing map
\phi^{I}_{\Omega}
being
\Phi_{C}
.

(II)

There is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of first type with the third fundamental form on
\partial\Omega
isometric to
h^{*}_{-}
, and the
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map
\phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
being
\Phi_{C}
.

(III)

There is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type with the induced metric on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
isometric to
h_{\pm}
, and the
I
-gluing map
\phi^{I}_{\Omega}
being
\Phi_{C}
.

(IV)

There is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type with the third fundamental form on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
isometric to
h^{*}_{\pm}
, and the
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map
\phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
being
\Phi_{C}
.

(V)

There is a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type with the induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) on
\partial_{-}\Omega
(resp.
\partial_{+}\Omega
) isometric to
h_{-}
(resp.
h^{*}_{+}
), and the
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-mixed gluing map
\phi^{(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)}_{\Omega}
being
\Phi_{C}
.

Proof.

We discuss the following two cases, according to the types of convex domains.

Case 1
: For the case of convex domains of first type (including the equivalence for Statements (I)-(II)), we just need to show the equivalence between Statements (i) and (I), since the equivalence between Statements (ii) and (II) will follow in the same way.

We first show that Statement (i) implies Statement (I). Let
\Omega
be the convex subset appearing in Statement (i). Since
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
, the induced metric on
\partial\Omega
is isometric to
h_{-}
. Recall that
\Phi_{C}=(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})
, where
\varphi^{\pm}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow D_{\pm}
is the biholomorphic map (that takes
0,1,\infty
to the same three points of
C
). Note that
\varphi^{\pm}_{C}
is well-defined up to left composition with a common Möbius transformation.

Set
\varphi^{+}:=\phi\circ\varphi^{+}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\Omega
and
\varphi^{-}:=\phi\circ\varphi^{-}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega
. By assumption,
\phi
is bi-holomorphic from
D_{+}
to
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
, then
\varphi^{+}
is bi-holomorphic. Since
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
, we have

(\varphi^{-})^{*}(I)=(\phi\circ\varphi^{-}_{C})^{*}(I)=(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{*}(\phi^{*}I)=(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{*}(h_{-}),

which is conformal to the hyperbolic metric
h^{-}_{0}
(this follows from Lemma
5.4
and the fact that
\varphi^{-}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow D_{-}
is bi-holomorphic and
h_{-}
is a conformal metric on
D_{-}
). Note that

(\partial\varphi^{-})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{+}=(\phi\circ\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\phi\circ\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})=(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})=\Phi_{C},

which is normalized. So
(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})
is a representative pair for the
I
-gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
of
\Omega
. Then there is an element
f_{1}\in\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
such that
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}=f_{1}\circ\varphi^{+}
and
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}=f_{1}\circ\varphi^{-}
.
Hence,

\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}=(f_{1}\circ\partial\varphi^{-})^{-1}\circ(f_{1}\circ\partial\varphi^{+})=\Phi_{C}.

Therefore,
\Omega
is the convex domain in Statement (I).

We now show that Statement (I) implies Statement (i). Let
\Omega
be the convex subset given in Statement (I). Recall that
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}
and
\Phi_{C}=(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})
.
By Statement (I),
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=\Phi_{C}
, then

(40)

(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})\circ(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}=\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})^{-1}.

We claim that
\Omega
is a desired convex domain for Statement (i). Set
\phi:=\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi_{C}^{+})^{-1}
on
D_{+}
and
\phi:=\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}
on
D_{-}
. By (
40
),
\phi
is continuous on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}=D_{+}\cup C\cup D_{-}
. Moreover,
\phi
is bi-holomorphic from
D_{+}
to
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
. It remains to show that
\phi^{*}I=h_{-}
on
D_{-}
. Note that

(41)

\phi^{*}I=(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1})^{*}I=((\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1})^{*}((\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{*}I).

By the definition of
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega
, it follows that
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{*}I
is conformal to
h^{-}_{0}
, namely,

(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{*}I=e^{2u_{I}}h^{-}_{0},

where
u_{I}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function. By assumption,
h_{-}
is a conformal metric on
D_{-}
, and
\varphi^{-}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow D_{-}
is bi-holomorphic. Then by Lemma
5.4
,

(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{*}h_{-}=e^{2u_{h_{-}}}h^{-}_{0},

where
u_{h_{-}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function. Since
I
and
h_{-}
are isometric by Statement (I), it then follows from Lemma
5.4
that the isometry between
({\mathbb{H}}^{2-},e^{2u_{I}}h^{-}_{0})
and
({\mathbb{H}}^{2-},e^{2u_{h_{-}}}h^{-}_{0})
is a bi-holomorphic map on
{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}
(also a hyperbolic isometry). So this isometry is an identity and therefore
u_{I}=u_{h_{-}}
.

Combined with (
41
), we obtain that

\phi^{*}I=((\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1})^{*}(e^{2u_{I}}h^{-}_{0})=((\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1})^{*}(e^{2u_{h_{-}}}h^{-}_{0})=((\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1})^{*}((\varphi^{-}_{C})^{*}h_{-})=h_{-}.

Case 2
:
For the case of convex domains of second type (including the equivalence for Statements (III)-(V)), we just need to show one of them, since the others will follow in the same philosophy.
We show the equivalence between Statements (iii) and (III) for instance.

We first show that Statement (iii) implies Statement (III). We can apply analogous analysis as Case 1. Let
\Omega
be the convex subset appearing in Statement (iii). Set
\varphi^{+}:=\phi\circ\varphi^{+}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}\rightarrow\partial_{+}\Omega
and
\varphi^{-}:=\phi\circ\varphi^{-}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial_{-}\Omega
. Let
I_{\pm}
denote the induced metric on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
. By Statement (iii),
\phi^{*}I_{\pm}=h_{\pm}
, so the induced metric on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
is isometric to
h_{\pm}
. Then

(\varphi^{\pm})^{*}(I_{\pm})=(\phi\circ\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{*}(I_{\pm})=(\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{*}(\phi^{*}I_{\pm})=(\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{*}(h_{\pm}),

which is conformal to the hyperbolic metric
h^{\pm}_{0}
(using Lemma
5.4
again). Note that

(\partial\varphi^{-})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{+}=(\phi\circ\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\phi\circ\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})=(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})=\Phi_{C},

which is normalized. So
(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})
is a representative pair for the
I
-gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
of
\Omega
. Then there is an element
f_{3}\in\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
such that
\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}=f_{3}\circ\varphi^{+}
and
\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega}=f_{3}\circ\varphi^{-}
. Hence,

\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=(\partial\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}=(f_{3}\circ\partial\varphi^{-})^{-1}\circ(f_{3}\circ\partial\varphi^{+})=\Phi_{C}.

Therefore,
\Omega
is the desired convex domain in Statement (III).

We now show that Statement (III) implies Statement (iii). Let
\Omega
be the convex subset given in Statement (III). Recall that
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=(\partial\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}
and
\Phi_{C}=(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})
.
By Statement (III),
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=\Phi_{C}
,
then

(42)

(\partial\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega})\circ(\partial\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}=\partial\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}\circ(\partial\varphi^{+}_{C})^{-1}.

We claim that
\Omega
is a desired convex domain for Statement (iii). Set
\phi:=\varphi^{I_{+}}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi_{C}^{+})^{-1}
on
D_{+}
and
\phi:=\varphi^{I_{-}}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi^{-}_{C})^{-1}
on
D_{-}
. By (
42
),
\phi
is continuous on
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}=D_{+}\cup C\cup D_{-}
. It remains to show that
\phi^{*}I_{\pm}=h_{\pm}
. Note that

(43)

(\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega}\circ(\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{-1})^{*}I_{\pm}=((\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{-1})^{*}((\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{*}I_{\pm}).

By the definition of
\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega}
, it follows that
(\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{*}I_{\pm}
is conformal to
h^{\pm}_{0}
, namely,

(\varphi^{I_{\pm}}_{\Omega})^{*}I_{\pm}=e^{2u_{I_{\pm}}}h^{\pm}_{0},

where
u_{I_{\pm}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function. By assumption,
h_{\pm}
is a conformal metric on
D_{\pm}
, and
\varphi^{\pm}_{C}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow D_{\pm}
is bi-holomorphic. Then by Lemma
5.4
,

(\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{*}h_{\pm}=e^{2u_{h_{\pm}}}h^{\pm}_{0},

where
u_{h_{\pm}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2\pm}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function. Since
I_{\pm}
and
h_{\pm}
are isometric by Statement (III), applying Lemma
5.4
again as above, it follows that
u_{I_{\pm}}=u_{h_{\pm}}
. Combining (
43
), we have

\phi^{*}I_{\pm}=((\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{-1})^{*}(e^{2u_{I_{\pm}}}h^{\pm}_{0})=((\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{-1})^{*}(e^{2u_{h_{\pm}}}h^{\pm}_{0})=((\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{-1})^{*}((\varphi^{\pm}_{C})^{*}h_{\pm})=h_{{}_{\pm}}.

∎

5.4.2.
Convex domains in quasifuchsian case

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
\rho_{-},\rho_{+}:\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
be two Fuchsian groups and let
f\in{\mathcal{T}}
be a quasifuchsian quasisymmetric homeomorphism which is
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
-equivariant. Let
h_{\pm}
and
h^{*}_{\pm}
be the metrics on
D_{\pm}
given above. Assume that
h_{-},h^{*}_{-}
(resp.
h_{+},h^{*}_{+}
) are
\rho_{-}
-invariant (resp.
\rho_{+}
-invariant). Then

(1)

There exists a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of first type, invariant under a quasifuchsian representation from
\pi_{1}(S)
to
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, with the induced metric on
\partial\Omega
isometric to
h_{-}
and the
I
-gluing map of
\Omega
being
f
.

(2)

There exists a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of first type, invariant under a quasifuchsian representation from
\pi_{1}(S)
to
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, with the third fundamental form on
\partial\Omega
isometric to
h^{*}_{-}
and the
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map of
\Omega
being
f
.

(3)

There exists a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type, invariant under a quasifuchsian representation from
\pi_{1}(S)
to
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, with the induced metric on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
isometric to
h_{\pm}
and the
I
-gluing map of
\Omega
being
f
.

(4)

There exists a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type, invariant under a quasifuchsian representation from
\pi_{1}(S)
to
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, with the third fundamental form on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
isometric to
h^{*}_{\pm}
and the
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map of
\Omega
being
f
.

(5)

There exists a convex domain
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
of second type, invariant under a quasifuchsian representation from
\pi_{1}(S)
to
\rm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, with the induced metric (resp. third fundamental form) on
\partial_{-}\Omega
(resp.
\partial_{+}\Omega
) isometric to
h_{-}
(resp.
h^{*}_{+}
) and the
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-mixed gluing map of
\Omega
being
f
.

Proof.

We show the proposition in two cases, according to the two types of convex domains:

Case 1
: For the case of convex domains of first type (namely Statements (1)-(2)), it suffices to show Statement (1), since Statement (2) will follow analogously.

Let
c_{+}
denote the conformal structure on
S
invariant under the action of
\rho_{+}
. Since
h_{-}
is invariant under the action of
\rho_{-}
, then
h_{-}
descends to a smooth, complete metric on
S
, say
\bar{h}_{-}
. By Corollary
1.3
, there is a unique (up to isometries) quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold
M
which contains a convex subset
N
with induced metric on the boundary
\partial N
isometric to
\bar{h}_{-}
and the conformal structure on the boundary at infinity
\partial_{\infty}N
conformal to
c_{+}
. We consider the lift of
N
in the universal cover (which is identified with
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
) of
M
and denote it by
\Omega
. Then the induced metric on
\partial\Omega
is isometric to
h_{-}
.

By Example
5.6
, the
I
-gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is a
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
-equivariant quasisymmetric homeomorphism. Note that
f:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is also a
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
-equivariant quasisymmetric homeomorphism. With the normalized condition of gluing maps
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
and the element
f
in the universal Teichmüller space
{\mathcal{T}}
, we have that
f=\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
.

Case 2
: For the case of convex domains of second type (namely Statements (3)-(5)), it suffices to show one of them, since the other statements will follow in a similar way.
We show Statement (4) for instance.

Note that
h^{*}_{\pm}
is invariant under the action of
\rho_{\pm}
,
h^{*}_{\pm}
descends to a smooth, complete metric on
S
, say
\bar{h}^{*}_{\pm}
. Applying Theorem
1.6
to the case that
n=2
with
M
homeomorphic to the product of
S
(homeomorphic to
\partial_{1}M
and
\partial_{2}M
) by
[0,1]
,
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
,
h^{*}_{1}=\bar{h}^{*}_{+}
and
h^{*}_{2}=\bar{h}^{*}_{-}
, there is a unique (up to isometries) quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold
M
which contains a convex subset
N
with the third fundamental form on the boundary
\partial_{\pm}N
isometric to
\bar{h}^{*}_{\pm}
. We consider the lift of
N
in the universal cover (which is identified with
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
) of
M
and denote it by
\Omega
. Then the third fundamental form on
\partial_{\pm}\Omega
is isometric to
h^{*}_{\pm}
.

By Example
5.8
, the
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map
\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is a
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
-equivariant (normalized) quasisymmetric homeomorphism. Since
f:{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{RP}}^{1}
is also a
(\rho_{+},\rho_{-})
-equivariant (normalized) quasisymmetric homeomorphism, we have that
f=\Phi^{I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI}_{\Omega}
.
∎

5.4.3.
Approximation of convex domains

We first recall a result of Labourie
[
25
, Théorème D]
, which will be used in the sequel.

Theorem 5.15

(Labourie)
.

Let
\iota_{n}:S\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
be a sequence of immersions of a surface
S
such that the pull-back
\iota^{*}_{n}(h)
of the hyperbolic metric
h
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
converges smoothly to a metric
g_{0}
. If the integral of the mean curvature is uniformly bounded, then there is a subsequence of
\iota_{n}
which converges smoothly to an isometric immersion
\iota_{\infty}
such that
\iota^{*}_{\infty}(h)=g_{0}
.

It deserves mentioning that Theorem
5.15
is local, in the sense that no assumption of compactness of
S
or completeness of
\iota^{*}_{n}(h)
is required.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of
(\rho^{+}_{n},\rho^{-}_{n})
-equivariant quasisymmetric homeomorphisms (where
\rho^{\pm}_{n}
are Fuchsian representations of a surface group
\Gamma_{n}
).
Let
h_{\pm}
be a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metrics of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
and let
(h^{\pm}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of
\rho^{+}_{n}
(resp.
\rho^{-}_{n}
)-invariant conformal metrics on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
. We first give the following statements concerning the convergence of convex domains of first type.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
(\iota_{n}:({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h^{-}_{n})\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of isometric immersions whose image together with a part of
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
bounds a convex domain of first type, which is invariant under a quasifuchsian representation of
\Gamma_{n}
with
I
-gluing map
f_{n}
.
Assume that

(i)

(\iota_{n}(0))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
is contained in a compact subset of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
,

(ii)

the maps
f_{n}
are uniformly quasi-symmetric,

(iii)

(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
converges to
f\in{\mathcal{T}}
in the
C^{0}
-sense, and

(iv)

(h^{-}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
converges
C^{\infty}
to
h_{-}
on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

Then
(\iota_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
has a subsequence which converges smoothly on compact subsets to an isometric immersion
\iota:({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h_{-})\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
whose image together with a part of
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
bounds a convex domain of first type, with
I
-gluing map
f
.

The proof of this proposition will use the following two lemmas.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
h
be a complete conformal metric on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
such that

•

h
is bounded, in the sense that there exists a constant
c>1
such that
h\leq ch_{0}
, where
h_{0}
is the hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
,

•

h
has curvature
K_{h}\in[-1,1/\epsilon]
for some
\epsilon>0
.

Then there exists
l_{0}>0
, depending only on
c
and
\epsilon
, such that the injectivity radius of
h
is at least
l_{0}
.

Proof.

If
h
admits a simple closed geodesic on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
. Let
\gamma
be a simple closed geodesic in
({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h)
of length
l
. Since
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
is simply connected,
\gamma
bounds a disk
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

Note that
h
is complete and has curvature at least
-1
. Therefore
h\geq h_{0}
(see
[
29
, Theorem 2.8]
). So the length of
\gamma
for
h_{0}
is bounded from above:
L_{h_{0}}(\gamma)\leq L_{h}(\gamma)=l
.

The hyperbolic isoperimetric inequality then implies that the area of
\Omega
for
h_{0}
is also bounded from above by

A_{h_{0}}(\Omega)\leq L_{h_{0}}(\gamma)\leq l~.

Since
h
is bounded, it then follows that

A_{h}(\Omega)\leq cA_{h_{0}}(\Omega)\leq cl~.

It then follows that the total curvature of
\Omega
for
h
is at most
cl/\epsilon
.

However the Gauss-Bonnet theorem implies that the total curvature of
\Omega
for
h
must be equal to
2\pi
, since
\gamma
is geodesic for
h
. Thus we finally obtain that

2\pi\leq\frac{cl}{\epsilon}~,

so that
l\geq 2\pi\epsilon/c
, and the injectivity radius of
({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h)
is at least
\pi\epsilon/c
. If
h
admits no simple closed geodesics on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, then its injectivity radius is naturally at least
\pi\epsilon/c
. This concludes the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
M
be a quasifuchsian hyperbolic manifold, and let
\Sigma\subset M
be a closed, locally convex surface embedded in
M
such that the induced metric
I
on
\Sigma

•

is bounded, in the sense that
I\leq ch_{-1}
, where
c>0
and
h_{-1}
is the hyperbolic metric conformal to
I
,

•

it has curvature
K_{I}\in[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
, where
\epsilon>0
.

Then there exists
r>0
such that all points of
\Sigma
are at distance at most
r
from the convex core of
M
.

Proof.

Let
I^{*}
be the metric defined in
\Sigma
as

I^{*}=\frac{1}{2}(I+2I\hskip-2.84544ptI+I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI)~,

where
I\hskip-2.84544ptI
and
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
are the second and third fundamental form on
\Sigma
, respectively. It is well-known that
I^{*}
is conformal to the pull-back on
\Sigma
of the conformal metric at infinity by the hyperbolic Gauss map, see
[
39
]
. We also denote by
h^{*}_{-1}
the hyperbolic metric on
\Sigma
conformal to
I^{*}
.

We first prove that there is a constant
c_{0}>1
such that

(44)

I^{*}\leq c_{0}h^{*}_{-1}.

This follows from the following points.

•

It follows from Lemma
5.17
that the injectivity radius of
I
is bounded from below by a fixed positive constant. It therefore follows from Lemma
4.6
that there exists a constant that bounds from above the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
.

•

As a consequence, there exists a constant
c_{1}>1
such that
(1/c_{1})I\leq I^{*}\leq c_{1}I
on
\Sigma
.

•

This implies that
I^{*}
is uniformly quasi-conformal to
I
, and it follows that there exists a constant
c_{2}>1
such that
(1/c_{2})h_{-1}\leq h^{*}_{-1}\leq c_{2}h_{-1}
.

•

By the assumption that
I
is complete with curvature
K_{I}\geq-1
and is bounded, we have
h_{-1}\leq I\leq ch_{-1}
.

Putting those inequalities together yields the desired inequality (
44
).

Now
\Sigma
is equal to the Epstein surface (see
[
18
]
) associated to the push-forward of
I^{*}
on one connected component of
\partial_{\infty}M
by the Gauss map. Equation (
44
) therefore implies that it is at distance at most
\log(c_{0})/2
from the Epstein surface of
h^{*}_{-1}
, which is known
[
5
]
to be within a uniform distance from the convex core of
M
. The result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.16
.

Note that since
h_{-}
is a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metric of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
, Lemma
5.17
shows that the injectivity radius of
h_{-}
has a positive lower bound.
Combining this with assumption (iv), there are constants
\epsilon>0,C>1
such that for
n
large enough the curvature of
h^{-}_{n}
is valued in
[-1+\epsilon/C,C/\epsilon]
and the injectivity radius of
h^{-}_{n}
is bounded from below by
1/C
.

Since
h^{-}_{n}
is isometric to the induced metric on
\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
. It therefore follows from Lemma
4.6
that the principal curvatures of
\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
are uniformly bounded between two positive constants. Combined with Theorem
5.15
and assumption (i),
\iota_{n}
converges smoothly on compact subsets to an isometric immersion
\iota:({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h_{-})\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. We denote by
\Omega_{n}
(resp.
\Omega
) the convex connected component of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\setminus\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
(resp.
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}\setminus\iota({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
), and denote by
I_{n}
(resp.
I
) the induced metric on
\partial\Omega_{n}
(resp.
\partial\Omega
), which is isometric to
h^{-}_{n}
(resp.
h_{-}
).

We claim that
\partial_{\infty}\Omega
is a closed quasi-disk in
{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
(which implies that
\Omega
is a convex domain of first type). To see this, note that the principal curvatures of
\partial\Omega_{n}=\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
are uniformly bounded between two positive constants (as shown above). This implies that the hyperbolic Gauss map say
G_{n}
from
\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
to
\partial_{\infty}{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
is uniformly quasi-conformal (note that
G_{n}
is a homeomorphism and its boundary map
\partial G_{n}
is the identity from
\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{n})
to itself).
Recall that the
I
-gluing map of
\Omega_{n}
is
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}}=f_{n}=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}
, where
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\Omega_{n}\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
is a bi-holomorphic map, while
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega_{n}
is a map such that
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{*}(I_{n})
is conformal to
h^{-}_{0}
. Let
\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}\setminus\partial_{\infty}\Omega_{n}
be a bi-holomorphic map such that
(\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}
is the conformal welding of the quasi-circle
\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{n})
. In particular, we have

\begin{split}f_{n}=\Phi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}}&=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}}\circ\big((\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}\big)\\
&=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial G^{-1}_{n}\circ\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}}\circ\big((\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}\big)\\
&=\partial\big((\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ G^{-1}_{n}\circ\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}}\big)\circ\big((\partial\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}\big)\end{split}

By the above discussion,
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ G^{-1}_{n}\circ\varphi^{c-}_{\Omega_{n}}
is a uniformly quasiconformal map (see e.g.
[
10
, Proposition 3.14]
) and its boundary map is thus a uniformly quasisymmetric homeomorphism. It then follows from hypothesis (ii) that the conformal welding of
\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{n})
is uniformly quasisymmetric, so by Theorem
5.3
, the
\partial_{\infty}\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})=\partial_{\infty}(\partial\Omega_{n})
are quasi-circles with a quasi-conformal constant which is bounded from above by a fixed constant.

The sequence of quasi-circles
\partial_{\infty}\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
, which are uniform quasi-circles, has a subsequence converging either to a quasi-circle or to a point. However, it cannot converge to a point, since otherwise the distance between
\iota_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
and the boundary of the convex hull of the corresponding quasifuchsian manifolds would go to
\infty
, contradicting Lemma
5.18
.

Since
\partial_{\infty}\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}P}^{1}
has more than one point, by Carathéodory’s theorem,
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
has a subsequence
(\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n_{k}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
converging to a bi-holomorphic map, say
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2+}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\Omega
. Combined with Lemma
5.4
and assumption (iv), the corresponding subsequence
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n_{k}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
converges to a map, say
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}:{\mathbb{H}}^{2-}\rightarrow\partial\Omega
, with
(\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{*}I
conformal to
h^{-}_{0}
. Since the gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}}=f_{n}=(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega_{n}}
is normalized, the map
(\partial\varphi^{I}_{\Omega})^{-1}\circ\partial\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}
, as the limit of
f_{n_{k}}
, is also normalized and is thus the
I
-gluing map
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}
of
\Omega
(combined with the above properties about
\varphi^{I}_{\Omega}
and
\varphi^{c}_{\Omega}
). By assumption (ii),
f_{n}=\Phi^{I}_{\Omega_{n}}
converges to
f
. As a consequence,
\Phi^{I}_{\Omega}=f
. The proposition follows.
∎

Note that a complete, bounded conformal metric on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
with curvature varying in
[-C,0]
with
C>0
has infinite injectivity radius. We have a dual version of Lemma
4.6
in the following:

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. There exists a constant
0<k_{0}<1
such that if
\Sigma\subset{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
is a spacelike convex surface with the induced metric of curvature varying in
[-1/\epsilon,0]
, then the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
are in
[k_{0},1/k_{0}]
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma^{*}
the surface dual to
\Sigma
in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. It has curvature
K^{*}=K/(1-K)\in[-1/(1+\epsilon),0]
. One can thus apply Lemma
4.6
to
\Sigma^{*}
, which therefore has principal curvatures in
[k_{0},1/k_{0}]
. The principal curvatures of
\Sigma
are the inverse of the corresponding principal curvatures of
\Sigma^{*}
, and the lemma follows.
∎

We now consider the corresponding convergence problem for convex domains of second type. Notice that Theorem
5.15
has an analog in the de Sitter space
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}

[
37
, Theorem 5.5]
and Lemma
5.19
holds. We list one case (for the
I
-gluing map) in the following for instance, since the cases for
I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI
-gluing map and
(I\hskip-2.84544ptI\hskip-2.84544ptI,I)
-gluing map can be stated and shown in the same manner, by considering the isometric immersions of the disk
({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h^{*\pm}_{n})
or
({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h^{*}_{\pm})
in
{{\rm d}\mathbb{S}}^{3}
instead.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
(\iota^{\pm}_{n}:({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h^{\pm}_{n})\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
be a sequence of a pairs of isometric immersions whose images bound a convex domain of second type, which is invariant under a quasifuchsian representation of
\Gamma_{n}
with its
I
-gluing map
f_{n}
. Assume that

(i)

(\iota^{\pm}_{n}(0))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
is contained in a compact subset
K^{\pm}
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
,

(ii)

the maps
f_{n}
are uniformly quasi-symmetric,

(iii)

f_{n}
converges to
f\in{\mathcal{T}}
in the
C^{0}
-sense, and

(iv)

(h^{\pm}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
converges
C^{\infty}
to
h_{\pm}
on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
.

Then
(\iota^{\pm}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
has a subsequence which converges smoothly on compact subsets to a couple of isometric immersions
\iota^{\pm}:({\mathbb{D}}^{2},h_{\pm})\rightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
whose images bound a convex domain of second type with its
I
-gluing map
f
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
5.16
. We include it here for completeness.

Note that
h_{\pm}
is a smooth, complete, bounded conformal metrics of curvature varying in
[-1+\epsilon,1/\epsilon]
on
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}
. Combining Lemma
5.17
with assumption (iv), there is a constant
C>1
such that the curvatures of
h^{\pm}_{n}
are valued in
[-1+\epsilon/C,C/\epsilon]
and the injectivity radius of
h_{\pm}
and
h^{\pm}_{n}
are bounded from below by
1/C
. Note also that
h^{\pm}_{n}
is isometric to the induced metric on
\iota^{\pm}_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
, it follows from Lemma
4.6
that the principal curvatures of
\iota^{\pm}_{n}({\mathbb{D}}^{2})
are uniformly bounded between two positive constants. Combined with Theorem
5.15
and assumption (i),
\iota^{\pm}_{n}
converges smoothly on compact subsets to an isometric immersion
